BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The Rev. H. Ogden Appointed Nonconformist Chaplain.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME PECULIAR QUESTIONS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday, when Mr. A. J. Jolley presided. The minutes of the House Visiting Com- mittee, which were confirmed, contained the following item: It was decided to advertise for an assistant resident medical officer at a salary of Xl5O per annum, with the usual residential allowances, such advertisement to be open, with no restriction as to sex. At the same meeting of the House Visiting Committee the half-yearly report of the chap- lain was considered,. and after hearing the Rev. W. Bracecamp, the recommendation as to the provision of -separate accommodation for reli- gious worship was fully discussed. The Clerk "presented a report showing the arrangements in force in a number of similar sized institu- tions in the North of England. Miss BROADBENT moved and Mr. GARNETT sec- onded that the Committee place on record their opinion that separate -accommodation for reli- gious worship should be provided at the Work- house, _and that they request the Board to take steps to give effect to that as soon as possible. On being put to the vote four voted for the resolution and four against. The Chairman (the Rev. A. M. Mitchell) gave 'his casting vote in favour of the motion, which was carried. The minutes were approved on the proposi- tion of Mr. BROOKS, the Rev. MITCHELL second- ing.

THE FARM PIGS.

Mr. BROOES commented on the fact that Mr. Mather and Mr. Lee had purchased several pigs, and asked "When are we going to have a report from the Farm Committee? I would like to know when Mr. Mather and Mr. Lee got their authority to purchase pigs. They did not get it from the House Committee, and certainly not from the Farni. Committee.' Rev. MITCHELL : How many are' there on the Farm Committee? Mr. BROOKS: I understand the pig purchas- ing Committee consisted of Mr. Lee and Mr. Mather. %hat has been dissolved and the Farm Committee was formed, and we have.had no minutes from them. The CHAIRMAN: One of the duties of the Com- mittee was the purchasing of pigs and dispos- ing of them in due course. I admit it is a little irregular. I cannot say why they have not reported to the House Committee. Mr. BROOKS: It seems high-handed on the part of some of the members to purchase pigs and spend about £lOO, and I enter a strong protest.

At a later stage in the meeting the matter was again discussed, and Mr. Brooks was in- formed that it was part of the duty of the Farm Committee to purchase the pigs. It was decided that a proper record of the proceedings of the Farm Committee be kept in future, Mr. Brooks having pointed out that they had not received a report from that committee since it was formed at the annual meeting.

The action of the Farm Committee members was confirmed.

FIRE BRIGADE PRACTICE.

In his report the Master stated that the Cor- poration Fire Brigade had made a surprise visit to the institute for practice. They arrived at the Workhouse within four minutes of re- ceiving the call.

It was pointed out that it was advantageous to know how soon the brigade could-get on the spot, and also that the men who were to con- stitute the Workhouse brigade had gained some valuable information as to the pressure of water in the mains and other matters.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

TRH EDITOR of "The Examiner" invites corre- spondence on all subjects of public interest. Lwrzzas should be brief and pointed. Their object must be to impart or seek information, net abuse. tartans must in all cases be accompanied by the name and address of the writer. COMMUNICATIONS received early in the week— except urgent matters—can always be given the best attention.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOULD SHEEP HAVE WATER?

Sir,—l would be 'extretnely obliged if you would kindly allow me through the medium of your columns to appeal to farmers and others to provide their sheep with drinking water. Unhappily, an impression is still prevalent in some parts of the country that sheep do not re- quire to be watered, and there is reason to be- lieve that at times flocks suffer considerably from want of drinking facilities. The opinions of eminent veterinarians are conclusive that the impression referred to is an erroneous one, and experiments by practical people have shown that sheep when allowed to get to water drink with avidity, and improve in condition. Some few years ago "The Field," writing on this subject, quoted a letter from a correspondent of the "Farmer and Stockbreeder," which I think may with advantage be brought to the notice of your readers at the present time. It is as follows "What dew falls may help to quench thirst, and succulent grass has a tendency that way, but none can be regarded as a substitute for water. I admit sheep have a wonderful capa- bility of doing without it, but I often noticed their anxiety to reach it, their- eagerness to drink when there, and their contentment when satisfied. Their extra bleating has been stopped with a drink, and their persistency to push through the fences and stray have been replaced by quiet submission to confinement in fields (where water was available. I believe letting sheep have access to water is of greater assistance to their well-being than is generally suppoed. Perhaps hill sheep give many an idea that they live without water, but those familiar with hills know there are many little springs and moist places which few can see, and I have generally found sheep footpaths lead to these in ways which suggested much intimacy with water." The words of the-writer are, I feel sure, a con- vincing answer to the question at the head of this letter, and in the interests of thirsty sheep, I appeal earnestly to all owners and others hav- ing charge of them, to make provision for a sufficiency of drinking water, more especially in hot weather, when their sufferings are greatest. EDWARD G. FAIRROLME, Chief Secretary, R.S.P.C.A.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
PARALYSED MARKETS.

Sir,—Ender the above heading and date of to- day you will find the following quotation in the "Daily News and Leader ": " Though a rise in the price of household com- modities may be expected, experts in food sup- plies state that the British housewife need have no fear of famine or even of famine Prices." Of course, we are very much obliged for the information; at the same time we realise that "it is more by luck than good management" that there is to be no famine. There is no war in England and there is no likelihood of war. There is every probability that no Englishmen will be called upon to fight, and yet the first. effect of the European war is to make Englishwomen suffer. For it cannot be denied that the slightest rise in food prices will be the cause of actual suffering and privation to many, and of worry and anxiety to a great many more women of this country, who are being told by their patriotic anti-suffrage fellow- men that women have no knowledge and no actual share in war.

Though no Englishmen may fall as a result of this war, so many poor little children in the slums of our great cities live on the verge of starvation that it is impossible to prevent the rise in the price of food (and bread will be the first thing to go up) forcing them over the brink. When will the day come—ivhen the value of things will be reckoned in lives and not in pro- plrty, as it is at present? Never, I say, until the women of England and the world are counted among the valuers, since it is only the women who can know the actual cost and value of a life.

D. B. PEMBERTON, Hon. Sec., Warrington Women's Suffrage Society. 90, Ellesmere-road, Stockton Heath, July 30, 1914.

OLD AGE PENSIONS.

The Board approved of the idea that the Guardians should co-operate with the Pension authorities in regard to the disqualification of persons receiving Old Age Pensions anti relief from the Guardians. Such a procedure would prevent persons from drawing the pension for the period they were in the Workhouse.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PADGATE 'BUS.

Three tenders were received for the convey- ance with a pair of horses, in which the Guard- ians could tgo to the Pidgate Homes. The amount of each tender was 11s., and it was de- cided to leave the matter with the Padgate Homes Committee.

In view of the- fact that Mr. James Dainteth had retired from his position as Registrar of the Rixton district, and the Registrar-General having previously informed the Board that in the cent of any such procedure the Rixton district should be amalgamated with Winwick, it was decided to write the Registrar-General Pointing out that such action would be a great inconvenience, considering the long distance from Rixton to Winwick.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 1, 1914.

The early hatched chickens are now rapidly approaching maturity; and they require special attention. Their feeding is a big factor towards success. A varied dietary is at all times bene- ficial, but never more so than in hot weather. It is well to consider what is required and the chief object to be gained.

Food must be in the direction of building up a big frame for the young stock, while for the old birds a strengthening illet is necessary to repair the waste after a heavy season's wor74 consequently fhr both old and young a high per- centage of albuminoids is required, and in this direction oats and wheat should form the staple grain, and bran and middlings, or bran and bean or pea meal, the soft food. Plenty of vege- tables and lean meat scraps, or fish meal, should not be omitted from their bill of fare, si.nce this is the best way of making up the albuminoid ratio.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

SUNLESS WEATHER AND A SLOW HARVEST.

Dull weather of the type common last week is well suited to the filling, but not to the colouring, of the grain and the ingathering of the crops, and in consequence the harvest has made slow progress, says "The Times." Cut- ting is general in many counties, the wheat and barley, having followed quickly upon the winter oats, but the absence of sunshine and drying winds has delayed carting longer than is good for the quality of corn and straw. No damage is reported up to the present, and the lower temperature diminishes risk of immediate de- preciation, but interruptions in the carrying of the cereal crops arcp regarded with anxiety. The results of extended inquiries as to the condition of the crops bear out the earlier esti- mate of exceptional irregularity. Scarcely two opinions that agree in the main particulars are obtained from any district. One farmer com- plains of limited bulk, another of, excessive luxuriance,' while of the intermediate reports • few suggest uniform excellence in all crops, and the ,wandering thunderstorms that have beenea characteristic of the July weather, as they were of that of June and May, explain the variable- ness of the harvesting experiences.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
DISEASE.

There were 93 outbreaks of swine fever in the week ended July 18, no fewer than 796 pigs hav- ing to be slaughtered. Other outbreaks of disease were: Anthrax, 13 (15 cattle, 5 pigs and 2 horses); glanders, G; parasitic mange, 21; 'Sheep scab, 2.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
SCARCITY OF STORE STOCK.

The feeling obtains that more attention will have to be given to the rearing of young com- mercial stock, says "The Times." The dairy farmer will prefer to sell his milk rather than rear calves so long as the market for the former continues, but the grazier must have stock from some source to eat his roots and hay. Those who adopt some form of mixed farming are begin- ning to recognise their obligations to themselves and their neighbours in the matter of. stock raising. Many are now buying, and • rearing calves, and even breeding them, who formerly relied upon external supplies, and as this prac- tice extends and its advantages are better under- stood the country will gradually attain a-posi- tion of greater security and independence.. .

===== 0002601_19140801_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
, IN THE GARDEN.

Stop vigorous grcowths of Vegetable Marrows, particularly those shoots 'upon which fruit is maturing. . _ _ _

Sow more Turnips, Carrots, Spinach, Rad- ishes, Onions, and Parsley. When outdoor Tomatoes have produced three trusses of' bloom, as soon as the fruit is set all growths should be stopped that the strength may go into them. The moment a crop 15 off clear away and burn the haulm or stems. The ground need not be dug over again, but lightly pricked over with the fork. If Cabbages, Sprouts, Kale, or Cauli- flowers are to be planted, rake a 'good dressing of lime into the surface.

The beautifully-marked scaly-backed insects, Ladybirds, beloved by the children, are most useful in the garden, as they feed on aphides and green fly/

In spraying, it is advisable to avoid disturb- ance as much as possible.

A sprinkling of Nitrate of Soda among plants of the Cabbage family, after a shower of rain, will have a good effect. Lettuce is also much improved by small dressings of this fertiliser. A few of the old-fashioned plants would-im- prove modern gardens, at least from an aromatic point of view. Mention may be made of Sweet Briar, Myrtle, Rosemary, Lavender, Thyme, Balm, Bergamot, Lemon-scented Verbena, and oak-leaved Geraniums, all of which possess a pleasant aroma.

Pinks may be propagated by the rough-and ready method of taking tufts or pieces of the side growths from off the old plants. The Polygonums;if not choice in blooms, are nevertheless decidedly ornamental and possess some decorative value. The blossoms are simi- lar to the Spimas. They are of a robust and vigorous habit, and are not particular as to position, often thriving in places where other plants would fail.

AN EASILY GROWN WATER LILY. One of the most beautiful, and, incidentally, one of the most easily grown of all Water Lilies is Nymphma Marliacea albida. It will make luxuriant leaves and produce a succession of blooms if given about three feet of water. It is one of the freest to bloom, and may be safely recommended for the small garden if given all the sun possible and a pool of still water. It is well known that all Water Lilies prefer still water to a running stream. The flowers are very large, pure white, with light yellow sta- mens, and are fragrant.—" The Garden."

HONEYSUCKLES IN THE GARDEN, It is indeed surprising that such a sweet- scented flower as the honeysuckle is not made more use of. Grown against a wall, it does not do itself justice, but as a hedge, a pole, or pillar plant, it is very desirable. Grown as a hedge it is beautiful, and given some support, the shoots soon coil themselves into a cable, which makes an excellent screen. Established as such, it re- quires but little attention—just a shortening of a few of the more extravagant growths is all that is necessary. There is another way in which honeysuckles may be grown, and which will probably appeal to a large number of gardeners. This is to grow is as a bush, and a very pretty effect is obtained. One or two stakes are required until a sym- metrical shape is reached, when it will support itself if necessary.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

GREAT SANKEY CLUB.

The Special Meeting.

A special meeting of the members and players of the Great Sankey Football Club was held at ! the Red Lion Hotel on Monday night, when, : owing to the inability of the Chairman, Mr. P. Cooper, to attend, Mt. J. Worsley occupied the chair. The hon. secretary, Mr. E. Clarke, pre- sented the balance sheet for the past season, which showed the' club to be in a very satisfac- tory position financially, and was unanimously accepted. Following this the most important business on the agenda was a discussion to decide which competition to enter for the com- ing season, and representatives were present fron. the new Widnes Association League and the Widnes and District Alliance, who were in- vited to express their views to the meeting, which was followed with much interest, and afterwards keenly discussed. . .

It n trs decido unanimously to again join the 'Warrington and District League, the players declaring themselves to have been very well treated by the Warrington .and District League, and they had no intentions to withdraw. Mr. Edwin Clarke, Oakleigh, Great 'Sankey, was thanked for his past services and re-elected to office as hon. secretary and -ton. treasurer. All last season's players are again available, and are anxiously looking forward to another successful season.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
A WARNING TO THE CHESHIRE HOUNDS.

Asked whether his attittlde towards the Che- shire Hounds would be the same in the coming season as it was in the last, Mr. W. Thomas, of Sandbach, declared that he intended to be on his grounds at Sprdston and Kinderton with his gun to shoot any fox he might see in the pres- ence of the hunting party. He would not allow the Cheshire Hounds .to cross his land if they gave him £5OO.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
CYCLING AND MOTORING. 1 THE CYCLISTS' NOTEBOOK.

Strides made in the Constructicn

of Tyres.

SAFETY 9F CYCLING.

So many people believe that 99 per cent. of all street accidents can be attributed to the cycle, that it is a pleasant experience to find someone in a responsible position who refuses to believe this, and moreover, who regards the pastime frbm a favourable point of view. Snell phrases—" The most dangerous, vehicle on the road !" "A positive menace to public safety r-- which often fall on our ears, though amusing enough in their way are apt to' come monoton- ous, particularly as they are quite devoid of' the saving virtue of truth. For my part, and' r have been a cyclist for a good many years now, the bicycle seems one of the safest modes elem. motion which exist. Such is evidently arse the opinion of the Chief Inspector of Police for the city of London. This official regards the number I of cycle accidents as very low, and, what ls more, considers the average city rider quite an expert. It is good to watch the wheelman threading his way in and 'out' of countless' motor 'buses, taxi-1 cabs, &c., without the slightest damage td him- self or anyone else. Speed-plus safety is char- acteristic of the cycle and that is what is re- quired in these days of traffic congestion. A Cycle Museum.

Taking into consideration the fact that so many ancient and modern cycles are to be seen on the roads, I have been thinking lately, that a permanent cycle museum would be both inter- esting and valuable. There are several other similar machines at South Kensington, but scat- tered and isolated bicycles give little idea of the enormous developments which have taken place in the now almost universal means of locomo- tion. As the motor museum at the Crystal Palace is an accomplished fact, an auxiliary cycle museum should not be a difficult matter, and I am convinced that in the event of a general appeal the relics which would be brought to light from thousands of cellars in the •kingdom would surpass all expectation. There is no reason why the exhibits should be limited to cycles; many accessories themselves have interesting histories, well worthy of exem- plification. Other suitable exhibits would be a typical machine of every year to show recent development, also sections of different working parts, with a short description of the principal manufacturing processes. I have recently heard a rumour that such a museum may 'become an accomplished fact in the near future.

Enjoyable Touring. ,I have heard from a number of correspondents telling me they fell fatigue after cycling, and desiring to know the cause. Perhaps the exCes- sively hot weather may have accounted for it this year, but all the blame cannot be attfiched to climatic conditions. I believe the whole an- swer lies in the one word "excess." There is no pastime more suited to all constitutions; the very young, the middle-aged, and even the veterans can enjoy its advantages, but on the other hand what outdoor recreation has been responsible for more strained hearts and under- mined constitutions? Every summer it is the commonest thing in the world for young men fresh from counter or desk to set off for an exact- ing tour, without the slightest preliminary training, and then they wonder why the end of the holiday finds them jaded and out of sorts. The most suitable daily run must necessarily vary according to the roads, weather conditions, and the age and constitution of the rider, but in cycling, at any rate, it is a good plan to en- courage laziness—never overwork and often underwork. Then a cycle tour will prove itself one of the most delightful ways of spending a holiday either at home or abroad. The Cost of Petrol. ,

I have had occasion from time to time to refer in this column to the many rumours that have been passing round to the effect that petrol will shortly be selling at a reasonable price. When one reads in the daily Press about the possibility of petrol being produced for lid. per gallon, the future looks rosy, but so far none of these rumours have materialised, and we are still called upon to pay heavily for our spirit., One imagines that petrol should be cheaper, therefore, I have been interested in reading various paragraphs that have appeared in the London and other Press to ffie effect that prices are being cut with respect to this present-day necessity. do not propose to enter into a dis- cussibn as to the rights of this price-cutting campaign, since I am finite content for the present to accept even the penny off the gallon that is now the rule. Every little helps, but if what I hear is true, it will not be very long before the price of petrol will drop to a point that is more reasonable in consideration of its real value. Usually, after a rate war the con- sumer is made to stand the costs Of the war, but let us hope that this case will prove an excep- tion. ,Rubbereand its Effect on Motoring.

A short while ago I had the opportunity of visiting the North British Rubber Company's mills at Edinburgh, and I found the few hours I spent there of extreme interest. When open- ing the Rubber Exhibition in London the other _

day, Prince Arthur of Connaught stated that i this s an age of rubber, as was the past decade one of steel. This was impressed most firmly on my mind by all I saw on the occasion of my visit. The uses of rubber and its applications in new industries are every day increasing, and it may lie said that in fifty years' time rubber will be used for purposes that are undreamt of to-day. As an instance of this, one exhibit at the Rubber Exhibition referred to above showed a rubber room, in which everything was of this material—furniture, ornaments, floor and wall coverings—everything with the exception of the electric light and the ink. We are, however, more concerned with the effect that the intro- duction of rubber has had on the cycle and motor industry. We owe much to the discovery of the solid rubber, then the pneumatic tyre.

Rubber versus Steel.

It was the heaviness of the steel tyres' that hindered the develiipment of road traction for so long, but with the introduction of the light motor vehicle, the rubber tyre industry might be termed the greatest aid to transport that the world had ever seen. To indicate the enormous demand for tyres for the tens of thousands of cars on the roads .to-day, I may mention that the Edinburgh works cover an area of 15 acres, and give employment to about 5,000 people. The next step may be the introduction of synthetic rubber, in which case the motor industry will make still more rapid strides. Anyone who re- members the old days of the solid tyred cycle will be able to appreciate'the difference between that article and the present-day pneumatic. The public, however, have short minds, and are apt to take everything for granted. To realise fully what an advance has been made it is necessary to cast one's thoughts back a few years. The strides that have been made in tyre construc- tion are great.

Royal Ruby Success. In the Blackburn District Motor Cycling Club's annual hill-climbing competition on Waddington Fell, near Clitheroe, the Royal Ruby added one more to the many laurels it has won this year. In the miniature motor cycle class for machines with a cylinder capa- city not exceeding 270 cubic centimetres, Mr. W. G. Guest was first on a 21 h.p. single-geared Royal Ruby light-weight. Not only was the Royal Ruby first in its dm.; making fastest time, but it won the aerial gold medal from standing start on a hill which is one mile long with a 1 in 6 gradient. This is one more proof of the wonderful hill-climbing capacities of the Royal Ruby light-weight which have been so clearly demonstrated this year.

PLACES WORTH SEEING.

Spits Hall, Rudyard Lalulliie—a-r to the North Staffordshire line at Rudyard, and over- looking the lake, this house is to be seen. High upon the rising ground at the far side of the lake and amid beautiful surroundings, Spite Halt stauds right in front of Rudyard Villa, and effectively mars the charming view which could be obtained from the villa windows. The eccentric. individual who ordered this ugly structure to be erected took this method of re- senting' some political difference or some fancied slight or injury done to him by the then owner Ittayard Villa. That the front view should be more' effectively spoiled the roof was carried to an unusual height, and to add insult to in- jury. among the ridges were placed numbers of griffins' headb—derils in stone—with' their grin- ning' and' diabolical faces turned towards the villa. The situation of Rudyard Villa must at one time' have been most enviable. It stands in the midk of tharminp, country, and commands an ertensive view over Rudyard Lake. The only" view now obtained from the front is that of the back premises of Spite Hall, which is separated' *our the former by little more than a marrow road. The hall is now used as a place of refreshment, &c., for the many visitors who avail' themservew of the charming spots around and about Rudyard.

HELVELLYN.

It does a. cyclist good, now and then, to spend a holiday out of the saddle. The week I spent at Ullswater and partly described last Satur- day, was such a holiday. The only time when I really lenge& for my steed was when we had three or Muir miles of a main road to tramp, our left hands occupied with the trying but necessary exercise of wafting fern fronds in front of our faces to keep the flies from settling on them. Oh, for a cycle! No flies can keep up with you tong, especially in a country of such rare, coasts. But this has nothing to do with the above title. A party of us, including three ladies, set off gaily up Grisedale on Wednesday, the 22nd, and found the weather cool but &he, and the going easy as far as the tarn at the head of the valley. Strange that although Helvellyn towers above you all the time, the summit cannot once be seen until the valley has been left. Above the tarn a steep zigzag, react& ttp the side of Dolly Waggon Pike, the southern end of Helvellyn—why so quaintly named', I cannot say. But every step brings into view new distances, new peaks, fresh lakes, and fhlry rewards one for the climb. This is the easiest and therefore the longest route to the top, a climb of six miles to get up 3,116 feet, 50 feet Tower than Scafell and only 450 short of the- summit of Snowdon. I think the view from the mountain is the finest of all. The coming dawn was, the treat. Striding Edge is the name of the spur we decided to tackle, partly because it was the most direct route to our teas. It is well named, for in a score of places a stride would throw you down into the valley on either side. All modern skirts, need- less to say, absolutely barred. It is an experi- ence that all visitors to the Lakes should make certain of, especially if a clear day can be ob- tained. This, however, is by ffo means some- thing- to take for granted. I have now stayed three times at Glenridding, and this is the first real opportunity I have had of climbing Hel- vellyn; it must be a weary grind if rain or cloud awaits one at the top.

GAPE WORMS.

There is not one poultry-keeper in a thousand who has not been troubled by gape worms among his chicks. These worms are picked up by the chicks, and Make their way into the wind-pip, causing the birds great distress. You see the poor little sufferers opening their mouths, yawning fearfully and stretching their necks in their efforts to breathe, and if not treated at once they will most probably be suffo- cated.

The only remedy is to extract the worms by means of a long stripped feather, dipped in tur- pentine, inserted gently into the wind-pipe and given a half-turn. The worms will adhere to the feather and may then be destroyed. The absence of green food means an undesir- able pale colour in the yolk of the eggs.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
HYGIENE OF THE FOWL HOUSE.

.Being permitted to breathe oxygenated air during the long nights means vigorous, healthy, productive fowls, better fertility, and •stronger chickens. When plenty, of ventilation (not draught) is given, space can be economised and more fowls housed, whilst healthier birds reward the fresh-air treatment. It may be added that the erection. of raised dropping or manure boards will also save the floor space. No chickens should be allowed to perch until they are fully four months old (etipecially the mas- sive breeds), and it is far better to scatter, a nice layer of peat-moss litter, or even ordinary chaff, than to allow. perches in the chicken- houses. Some poultry-keepers are very remiss in such welters, and overlook the question of' crooked and ill-formed breastbones until it is too late. When the perches are fixed up (they should always be made movable for purposes of cleansing and disinfecting),it is important that they should be of good width, fiat ones being preferable to round ones; tiling battens, with the edges nicely planed, make excellent perches, and they should be,saturated with creosote or paraffin.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MALINES FOWL.

The 3/alines fowl is called after the district of that name in the Province of Brabant, and it is one of the most valuable of the Belgian varie- ties. Bred primarily for its table qualities, it is still a very good layer, and when the chickens are brought out at the right time of year the hens produce quite a large proportion of their eggs during the winter months. The flesh is creamy-white and of excellent flavour and tex- ture, but there is too much of the thighs for the breed to be classed as a really first-class table variety. They are large and lazy birds, and fatten very rapidly. It is stated by one authority that they grow at the rate of 211 b. per month after the first few weeks. It is an exceedingly hardy fowl, and the chickens can be raised successfully under the most adverse con- ditions. The hens are reliable sitters and mothers, but somewhat clumsy- The Malines is of the Asiatic type,.resembling in shape and general appearance the Brahma or Cochin. It is large and deep in body, with a prominent breast-bone and a straight keel. The neck is short, well curved, and thickly covered with feathers; the head is strong, with a red- dish-white beak, and orange eye, a single comb and long wattles. The comb is small and stands quite upright; it is, in fact, very like that of the Langshan. The wattles, comb, face, and ear- lobes are red. The wings are short, small and carried quite close to the body. The thighs and shanks are long; the/former are covered with a soft cushion, while the shanks and feet are feathered on the outer side. The feet are strong, and there are four toes; the legs and feet are pinky-white. The weight of an adult cock is from 91b. to 1111 b., and that of an adult hen from 811). to I.olb. ,

NEW DIAMOND RUSH IN SOUTH AFRICA.

A new rush for diamonds has taken place at Bloemhof in the Transvaal Province. Over two hundred claims have been pegged off at the lime kilns, and, it is stated that it is likely to prove one of the richest patches of diamondiferous ground. One stone weighing 1l carats and of good colour was found, and it is rumoured that another one weighing 221 carats has also been discovered,

ALDERMAN FORS)-(,&W DEAD.

Bluecoat Schoolboy Who Became Mayor.

A FINE RECORD.

now be in the capable hands of Mr. W. B. Forshaw.

WORK FOR THE TOWN.

Mr. Forshaw, who was a Conservative of the broad-minded type, entered the Town Council as the representative of Town Hall Ward in 1900. In 1906 he was elected an alderman, be- ing assigned to Orford Ward, in succession to the late Alderman John Roberts. The year 1908 witnessed the attainment of the height of hiss ambition, for his ambition to become Mayor of his native town was gained. He was made Mayor, and the honour was repeated the fol- lowing year. In 1909 he had the honour of receiving the late King Edward at .tbe Town Hall when that monarch passed through the town, accompanied by Queen Alexandra and Princess Victoria. Last year he was presented to King George and Queen Mary, and so had the distinction of being one of five—Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall and Mr. and Mrs. Arthur H. Crosfield being the other four—who were presented to their Sovereign at both visits.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
RESPONSIBLE FOR A WIDE BRIDGE.

Perhaps the greatest work Alderman Forshaw did for the town was in connection with War- rington Bridge, which unfortunately he has not lived to see completed. He was the great advocate, in season and out of season, for the erection of a bridge 80ft. wide. He spent much 7--.14, supporting ihe Bill for the bridge. It is largely owing to his persistence that towards the end of this year the town will .be possessed of .a bridge that will-be the widest in the provinces, and will be exceeded by only one in London, the Westminster Bridge, which is 82ft. wide. Those who are best able to judge believe that the growth of Warrington will justify this great link between the two counties of Lancaster and Chester, and that Alderman Forshaw's foresight will be fully vindicated. It was also largely due to his action, as Chairman of the Small-holdings Committee, that the allotment gardens in Victoria Park were increased from 20 to 48, and the fact that they are all fully cultivated and occupied by diligent and successful gardeners is proof that he was right in fighting as he did the opposi- tion which was brought to bear upon the pro- posal to make this use‘of a few acres of the town's land. .

He was a member of all the important com- mittees of the Council, andbdid his duty by them all.

AN ACTIVE CHURCHMAN.

In 1881 Mr. Forshaw married Miss Betty Baxter Barlow, the daughter of a well-known Warringtonian. He leaves a son, Mr. W. B. Forshaw, and two daughters, Miss Bessie For- shaw and Miss Edith Forshaw. Alderman Forshaw was through life a staunch and active member of the Church of England. He sang in the Parish Church choir, and sub- sequently became the president of the Parish Church Choristers' Society. He was connected with St. Paul's, and conducted services in an old barn, which served as a mission before. St. Barnabas' Church was built, and in the new church he frequently read the lessons. For many years he attended Trinity Church, where he was a churchwarden, but removing to Latch- ford he became attached to Christ Church, and was made churchwarden. He was vestry clerk for St. Luke's Church.

He was a Past Master of the Masonic Lodge of Charity. Among other offices and positions Mr. Por- i shaw held or had held were the following:— I President, Warrington Cricket, Bowling, and Tennis Club; president, Bluecoat Society; presi- dent, Law Clerks' Society; governor of the Grammar School as one of the representatives of the Town Council; member of the Board of Management and life governor of the Warrin—g- ton Infirmary.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUNERAL.

An Impressive Service.

The remains of the deceased gentleman were interred at Rill Clifie burial ground on Tues- day afternoon, in the presence of a large num- ber 4,, relatives; private and business friends, and representatives of numerous Warrington institutions. As the cortege slowly made its way along the road from Ivy Ifount to Christ Church, Wash-lane, it was noticeable that Mr. Forshaw was held in great respect by the townspeople, and that the serious nature of their loss was fully realised. At Christ Church, where the cortege was met by the Town Council and Corporation officials, who had been conveyed there in a motor 'bus, placed at their disposal by the Chairman of the Electricity and Tramways Committee, the Rev. G. C. Briggs, vicar, conducted a short service, whilst Mr. Fry presided at the organ, render- ing Chopin's Funeral March, "0 rest in the Lord," and the Dead March in "Saul" in an impressive manner. Preceded by a detachment of the local police, and tn the presence of a large crowd of friends, the funeral procession then proceeded to the graveside, where the Rev. G. C. Briggs again officiated.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====

MAGISTERIAL REFERENCE.

Prior to the business being commenced at the Police Court on Saturday morning the Chairman (Mr. R. T. Fairclough). said Alder- man Forshaw was a specimen of what might be done by determination and perseverance. He was a good citizen, and in his, the speaker's opinion, the greatest of his works was ,in, con- nection with the new Warrington bridge. It would be a lasting tribute to his memory if something could be erected on it as a tribute to him and his worth.

Mr. E. A. White (deputy-magistrates' clerk) said that on behalf of the magistrates' clerk, and as a•member of the profession to which the late Alderman belonged, he expressed his deep regret, and could also speak in appreciation of his character as a man. He could also say how much his profession would sympathise with Alderman Forshaw's family in the loss they had sustained.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE ALDERMANIC VACANCY.

The vacancy in the aldermanic bench will be reported at the next meeting of the Council, on August 11, and the Mayor will decide, when the vacancy shall be filled. The elevation of a councillor to fill Alderman Forshaw's place._ will mean a by-election in one of the wards.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
A MOSQUITO STING AT BISLEY."

Death of a London Police Chief Inspector. The death of Chief Inspector Rolfe, of Can- non-row Police Station, is attributed to a mos- quito sting at Bisley, a fortnight ago. Nothing serious was anticipated, but blood-poisoning supervened, and he was taken to St. Thomas' Hospital. Here he remained for several days in an unconscious condition, and died on Sun- day evening. He was a finely built man, and a very noticeable figure when Royal ceremonies were taking place. This death once again called attention to the danger, little recognised in England, lurking in the bite of the gnat or mosquito.--the blood- sucking branch of the order diptera. These in- sects are carriers of disease, especially in hot countries. They get a thirst for blood in Eng- land and even in tie Arctic circle during brief spells of hot weather, they bite with the most disastrous, if not always fatal, effect.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
People Killed and Wounded After Gun-Running

Exploit.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
UNAUTHORISED USE OF FORCE.

Terrible Sunday Scenes in the Irish Capital.

There was a lamentable collision between the military and police on the one hand and the Nationalist Volunteers and the people of Dublin in the neighbourhood of the Irish capital and in the city itself en Sunday. There were two conflicts, the outcome of a gun-running exploit by the Irish Volunteers at Howth on Sunday morning. From a private yacht the Nationalists landed, it is said, over 2,500 rifles and 170,000 rounds of ammunition. They cut the telegraph wires and stopped communication with Dublin, nine miles away. Motor-cars awaited the arrival of the yacht in, the little harbour, and conveyed the rifles and ammunition to Dublin. They were stopped at Clontarf by the King's Own Scottish Borderers and the police, and some firing oc- curred. The troOps seized only a few of the rifles.

When the soldiers and police marched back into Dublin they were followed by a crowd, mostly lads and women, who cheered for Mr. Redmond and Home Rule. Some stones were thrown, and at Bachelor's-walk, near the O'Connell Bridge, the troops turned and fired on the crowd.

A great many rifles were safely got away, and on Sunday night many Volunteess and others were carrying rifles in the streets of Dublin. Three persona were killed and about 80 were wounded'. The casualties included women and children.

Feeling against the regiment—the King's Own Scottigh Borderers—was so high that the men were confined to barracks preparatOry"to being drafted out of the city.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
AN UNAUTHORISED ACT. The Facts Laid Before tile House of Commons.

In the House of Commons on, Monday Mr. John Redmond, the Irish Nationalist leader, who was received with loud cheers, asked the Chief Secretary for Ireland whether he Is in a posi- tion to give the House full information as to the shocking occurrences in Dublin on Sunday; by whose order the military fired on the people; whether the Riot Act was read; and whether there was a magistrate present. (Ministerial and Nationalist cheers.)

===== 0002601_19140801_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. BIRRELL'S STATEMENT.

Mr. Birrell replied: The Assistant-Commis- sioner of the Dublin Metropolitan Police telephoned to the Under-Secretary's Lodge about 2 p.m. on Sunda that arms were being landed from a yacht at Howth, and that he had already communieated with the constabulary and the coastguards. The Under-Secretary, Sir James B. Dougherty, arranged with the Assistant-Commissioner to meet him at the Castle, and Sir James reached the Castle about 2.45. He then sent for the Assistant -Commissioner and the District Inspec- tor-Gengral of the Royal Irish Constabulary, the latter of whom came at once and told the Under-Secretary that a large body of men had left Howth carrying the arms which had been landed.

The Assistant Commissioner did' not appear at the Castle at all, lie had gone to consult the military. The Superintendent of the Dub- lin Metropolitan Police came from the office to say that the Assistant-Commissioner, was sorry he was unable foo Stay and see the Under-Secretary. The Under-Secretary en- deavoured to find where the Assistant-Commis- sioner was, and in the meantime he prepared the following minute, which he sent to the office of the Dublin Metropolitan Police:— "Assistant-Commissioner,—As regards the steps which you have taken on your own re- sponsibility to deal with the arms landed at Howth this morning, his Excellency is advised that forcible disarmament of the men now marching into Dublin with these arms should not, in all the circ.nmstances be attempted; but the names of the men carrying arms should. as far as possible, be taken and watch should

===== 0002601_19140801_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
ON HIS OWN RESPONSIBILITY.

The military were requisitioned by the As- sistant-Commissioner entirely on his own re- sponsibility, and for the purpose of preventing the Nationalist Volunteers marching into the city, which, he wa§ satisfied, could not be pre- vented unless the police were supported by an armed force of some kind. The Assistant-Com- missioner has been suspended pending an in- quiry. (Ministerial and Nationalist cheers.)

===== 0002601_19140801_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
THE YACHT.

Mr. Birrell, continuing, said: About 1 p.m. on Sunday a yacht landed arms at Howth. The arms were received by a large number of man, exceeding 1,000, who set off to march into Dublin through Clontarf. At Clontarf they were met by a body of Dublin Metropolitan Police and 160 men of the King's Own Scottish Borderers. The police tried to disarm the men in front, which was done with a struggle. The casualties at this spot appear to have been three Volunteers and two soldiers (one of whom was shot through the knee and another through the ankle), and one police- man. The soldiers did not fire.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIERS FIRED WITHOUT ORDERS.

The soldiers then returned to their barracks, and an attack was made on them with missiles by an angry crowd, principally in Bachelors- walk, between O'Connell Bridge and the Metal Bridge. Major Haig (King's Own Scottish Bor- derers) took over command in Talbot-street, and was himself struck five times with missiles. Major Haig, who was in front of the column, expostulated with the crowd, who were im- peding the soldiers. As he was speaking several soldiers in the rear of the column, becoming exasperated, fired without orders. Thirty-one rounds in all were fired, almost simultaneously. The officers succeeded in stopping the firing immediately, but casualties amounting to three killed and 32 injured had already occurred, and a con- siderable number of the soldiers had by this time received severe injuries. A full inquiry into the conduct of the military will be held at Once. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19140801_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
IRISH DEMANDS.

The question was raised, when Mr. Redmond practically made five demands 1. That Sir John Ross, Chief Commissioner of Police, should be suspended. 2. That there should be an inquiry by inde- pendent persons into the whole circumstances of the affair. _

_ 3. That there should he a full judicial and military inquiry into the action of the soldiers, and that proper punishment should be inflicted on them if they were guilty. 4. That the King's Own Scottish Borderers should be forthwith removed from Ireland.

5. That the Importation of Arms Act should be revoked. To these demands the following replies were given 1. Granted.

2 and 3. Full inquiry promised.

4. The conduct of the soldiers was a matter for the inquiry. "from which the Government anticipate the troops will emerge with credit." 5. Refused.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====

No attempt was made to interfere with them, nor were they asked to surrender their rifles.

AMENDING BILL POSTPONED.

The Amending Home Rule Bill, which was to have been taken in the House of Commons on Tuesday, was postponed indefinitely.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE SCOTTISII BORDERERS.

A correspondent writes to the "Manchester Guardian": Both battalions of the King's Own Scottish Borderers are very largely re- cruited from the Belfast command and also from a section of the Glasgow Irish population. In the Orange districts around Belfast there is reluctance to enlist in any of the Irish infantry iregiments, with the exception of the Innis- killing Fusiliers and the Royal Irish Riles, and intending recruits from this quarter usually enlist in Scottish regiments, the "K. 0.5.8. beitg a prime favourite. The result is that the regiment has, rightly or wrongly incurred *be suspicion of an Orange tinge, and this would doubtless explain why so much feeling was shown against them by the populace. The regiment is one of those that is in bad odour with Belfast Nationalists, and when stationed in that city it has been kept out of the Nation- alist quarter as much as possible.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON REFERENCES.

The Incident Described as a "Crime."

A NATIONALIST'S VIEW.

Reference was made to the affray in Dublin by the speakers at the meeting of the Progres- sive Labour League held at Bridge Foot on• Monday evening. The chief speaker was Coun- cillor Kendall, from Manchester, and the chair- man was Mr. J. Elwell. The Chairman said every right-thinking per- son would regret what had taken place in Dublin. They hoped the men responsible for setting in operation such revolutionary methods had been present in the fray to suffer, instead of the working people who had been made the

victims. (Applause.) When such things occurred it was always the workers who suffered. Those responsible for the setting in operation such methods stayed at home out of danger, while less fortunate people were made to suffer. (Applause.) The time must come when such would not be possible. (Applause.)

TWENTI ETH CENTURY CIVILISATION. Councillor Kendall said he had come prepared to speak about land and housing, but the events in Dublin had made him to change his mind. Still he could see a close connection between land and housing and what had happened in Dublin on Sunday. He asked all working men to realise the fact, which was not merely super- ficial, but very far-reaching. Even in the civil- ised twentieth century they founa that certain people could do things the majority of the people were not allowed to do, and the privileged people were those privileged for centuries—the class.

Even at this period they were fighting a great battle for the democracy, and the only way the mass could get justice was for th'e democracy io get absolute control of the governing of them- selves. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140801_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
RIGHTS OF DEMOCRACY.

Until they settled that—and they were going to settle it—it was no use talking about reform, about the miserable and wretched conditions under which the common people work and live, or -talk about anything else, they had first to settle whether the people shall rule, and when democracy started to rule such scenes as those in Dublin would be impossible. (Applause.) He was glad to see that the Assistant Com- missioner in Dublin had been suspended pend- ing a very searching inquiry, and that the cele- brated regiment which had gained so much notoriety had been banished to the Curragh Camp. (Applause.) His sympathy went out to the unfortunate people in Dublin who had been the victims of that crime—a crime that had been instigated and carried through for a purpose, and he hoped the real persons who instigated that etime would be promptly brought to book. (Applause.) It was time the power of the ascendency class was broken for ever. (Applause.) The fight to break the century old power of the class was going on and would continue to go on until democracy won. (A Voice: "Rotten !") A little millionaire down there said "rotten.'! (Laughter.) From the look of him it would appear as if he owned Warrington. (Laughter.) Land and wealth belonged to the class. Of the land, some 14 million acres were taken from the people under the Enclosure Acts, passed by landlords to rob the people. The landlords were now fighting to maintain their hold of the land which democracy threatened to take from them. The day of land reform was coming fast, and it behoved every worker to help on the coming of that day. The majority of the workers would do so, but there lwas a danger they had to face, and that was the man who went and deliberately voted against the interests of his own class. (Applause.)

" RUBY " MARGARINE

A TRUCE.

Mr. ASQUITH, in view of the trouble thr9atening on the Continent, an- nounced in the Commons on Thiirs- day that he proposed to postpone for the present the discussion on the Home Rule Amending Bill so that the nation could suspend domestic differences and present a united front to the world. The heads of the Opposition chival- rously fell in with the arrangement, and the voice of controversy is silent in the face of a common danger. It has been suggested that the danger may expedite a settlement of the Ulster question, and we. -hope that may prove to be true. The Govern- ment, however, are pledged to go on with the programme which is so near completion. The spirit in which the shooting upon Nationalists in Dublin has left Liberals and Irishmen was re- flected at the great London rally on 'Wednesday night, when it was, with unanimity, resolved to demand " that the Government shall complete their legislative programme and thus secure the effective operation of the Parlia- ment Act." Mr. JOSEPH DEVLIN, who is entitled to speak in, the name of Ireland generally and in the name of more than half of Ulster itself, summed up the situation neatly. He said Sir EDWARD CARSON and his friends are not fighting for Ulster, but for Toryism, and were living on reli- gious.hatreds, racial rancours, and the bitterness and bad feeling fomented in the vain hope that they may crawl back again 'to the responsibilities of office. As . the Nationalists, he said they had attended the meeting as part- ners in democratic glory, and the pres- ence of the Parliament Act an the Statute Book.was due in great measure to the unwavering support which the Irish party had given to the Radical party. They had co-operated with them in securing that measure of British liberty, and they would co-operate with them in maintaining it. They had set themselves to carry constitu- tional liberty for Ireland, religious equality for Wales, and democratic enfranchisement for England. This is the programme the Government will carry through before they go to the country. But further than to remind ourselves exactly where we stand, we do not wish to say anything at the moment that would seem contrary to the true British stand that is to be made, facing the world, temporarily free, at all events, from all domestic bickering.

FOR TARIFF REFORMERS IN DISMAL MOMENTS.

"Twenty years ago the international commerce of this country—then the greatest in the world—had attained the gigantic figure of £681,000,000. Last year it reached the still more startling figure of £1,400,000,000. In twenty years we had doubled the greatest in- ternational trade in the world. I think it is well to point out for our good cheer that half that increase occurred during the last five or six years. Twenty years ago the clearances of the London banks came to £6,478,000,000. Last Year they reached the incredible total of £16,426,000,000. This year they reached a still higher figure. That is, in twenty years they have trebled. That is a, fact which aught to cheer us up in dismal moments."—The Chancellor of the Exchequer in the City of London, July 17th. BACON 18 Dwunt.--Advt.

THE EXAMINER,

.Y, AUGUST 1, 1914.

PASSING WEEK. WOMEN LIBERALS AT BLACKPOOL,

MR. ARTIIUR CROSFIELD AND TIIE STRIKERS.

The tanners' strike collection started on the Football Ground last Saturday by Mr. Harold Smith realised over £26, and other sums have been received as per the list we publish to-day. There is no doubt that a generous response will be made to the Mayor's appeal, and that the legal disabilities of the Education Committee .during the holidays will be neutralized. Mr. A 'hur Crosfield who, at the highly suc- cessful L.beral Women's excursion to Blackpool on Thursday, referred sympathetically to the tanners' strike and the lamentable fact that a settlement has not been accomplished, besides contributing £lO to the fund which the Mayor opened with the object of feeding the children who are suffering through the dispute, has, we understand, undertaken to place £lO a week at the disposal of the Mayor while the strike lasts, to be used by 'his Worship to the best advan- tage in the alleviation' of distress in the homes of the strikers.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAMMAR SCHOOL AND THE AVERAGE

BOY.

Grammar School Speech Day on 'A nursday found the Head Master in a position to report that the efficiertcy so characteristic of the school has been fully maintained. The Rev. Horace Gray admitted that they did not look to produce prodigies, but to send forth good average boys for intelligence and physique, which, we think, is an admirable ambition and well calCulated to bring in a large crop of "old boys' honours" in after years, honours in which the school continues, we are glad to see, to be remarkably rich.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
A GARDEN PARTY : :THE MAYOR AND

THE CRIPPLES.

Whatever may be the defects of the Town Hall—and there are many from a civic point of view—the uses to which the present Mayor and Mayoress are applying it are commendable in the highest degree. On Thursday afternoon it was, with the delightful lawn at the back of the building, put to a most noble and praiseworthy use. Upwards of three hundred and fifty guests, , whose lives are not spoilt in pleasant places and ! are unfortunately marred by physical infirmi- ties, were present at. a Garden Party through the kindness of the Mayor and Mayoress. As I! they -assembled there was a new light on their ! faces, and like hearts. newly awakened to love and happiness, and throbbing with anticipation, it must have given their host and hostess unbounded pleasure to receive them. For once, the weather was propitious, and for two and a half hours they forgot their weaknesses, and sunshine and songs and music, and a repast of inviting dainties made the time pass altogether too quickly. That lawn—where every Bower and bird and bee told of unseen goodness—was a reminder that happiness can be brought into many lives by a little thoughtfulness and sacri- fice by those in high places. Never before, we believe, have such humble guests been invited to a garden party in Bank Park Gardens. It may have been for lack of thought and nothing more. The present Mayor and Mayoress have created a few , precedents during their short term of office, but there will ! be none, we venture to say, that will be remem- bered longer, and redound more to their credit, than that created on Thursday afternoon.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
SUNDAY FISIIING .AT LYNIM

"The Village" is agitated now on the ques- tion of fishing in the Dam on Sundays, as will be seen from our detailed report of Monday's meeting of the Council. Those in favour seem to have made it a question of "levelling up" for the working classes as against those tit the more leisured, and moneyed classes, who can; and do, golf on Sunday. We have received several letters on the subject, protesting vehe- mently against the desecration of the Sabbath, and asking the members on the Council who are active religious workers, including the Rector, to assert themselves.

Mr. Ogden admitted that he had never been so flattered, but added that he had been told he resembled Napoleon! . •

OF IRISH PICNICS.

"Sub Rosa" of "Daily News " fame has been showing, by means of a letter fm Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., how the trip business to Ireland is run. We have been privileged to reproduce this article, which all should carefully read, con- firming as it does every word of the revelation we made of the first trip that went from War- rington in January. Our readers will see that we also give a report which has been made by some real Liberals who were sent from Croydon and declined to be tied to the official programme and report. The result is a recognition of the demand for Home Rule, fair play for the Nationalist, and a severe rebuke for those who in the peculiar cir- cumstances of the case have seen fit to traduce the majority of the Irish people.

• MORE FROM MR. ENGLAND. Last week a- - letter . appeared in •tl "Daily Mail" from Mr. Ebenezer England, who was given the honour of the top of a column and headed "Another Unbeliever in Ireland." We have not room to repeat all Mr. England's epistle; perhaps our readers would neller forgive us if we did. But the sort of stuff he and those who pay the " exes" are still serving up for unsuspecting victims may be gathered from the following choice quota- tions :

Sir,—During the last six months I have travelled up and down Ireland and have covered more than 1,000 miles. My object was to study the people, their conditions,

and Blackpool was reached shortly before 10 o'clock. Here the women dispersed, and it was not long before a large majority of them were enjoying themselves to the full at South Shore, where the attractions are so many and varied. Mr. Crosfield, Mrs. Crosfield (president), as well as Miss Truda Crosfield ,(hon. treasurer) and Mrs. Osborne (the hon. secretary) were present, and enhanced their popularity in many respects. During the afternoon a member of the Associa- tion met with an accident on one of the tobog- gans, and Mrs. Crosfield attended to the injury with that tenderness and gentleness with which all in _Warrington are familiar. An excellent tea was served at Messrs. Clarke and Heap's restaurant, Church-street, at which there were many signs of enthusiasm and whole- heartedness in the cause. , The return journey was commenced about 9.30, and Warrington was reached about 11 o'clock.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
and the Home Rule question on the spot. - -

Those of us who have seen Mr. England in Warrington "during the last six months," and have held converse with him must have been very badly deceived. We must have interviewed his ghost and not known it. It is clearly a case for the society which investigates such mysteries, from a scientific point of view. Without visiting Ireland and mixing with the people, I have no hesitation in saying one is not capable of forming a correct opinion on the Home Rule question. I go further and say that I doubt very much if certain members of our Cabinet understand it.

No doubt these Cabinet Ministers will take the, Matter to heart. They would be well ad- vised in these busy times if they got to know from Mr. England how they could spend six months in Ireland and still remain at their posts in London. In the course of modern hustling one often sighs for the discovery of a method to be in two places at once. • Again, Prior to my visit to Ireland I was in favour of Home Rule, and as a matter of fact both worked and voted for the Liberal Party for over twenty-five years.

How Mr. England has voted is entirely a matter affecting his own conscience, and it should remain his own secret. Why he should protest so much about it is not quite apparent. We do know for a fact that for years "prior to my visit to Ireland," he was a declared Tariff Reformer, which is a long way removed from being. a Liberal and a Liberal worker. Tariff Reform is the rottenest plank in a rotten plat- form, and the man who, having ostentatiously proclaimed himself a Tariff Reformer, can con- tinue years after to pose as a Liberal may easily step on to the next rotten plank and proclaim himself an enemy of the democracy of Ireland. PROVISIONS ARE DEARER.—Advt.

A Splendid Day's Outing in Glorious Weather.

A FORMIDABLE ORGANISATION.

When an organisation caters for two sides— the educational and recreative—it is bound to be successful, and so it is with that very important body, the Warrington Women's Liberal Associa- tion. Whether it be in the more active sphere of politics, or in the enjoyment of a little re- laxation, the women who constitute this great asset to the Liberal party in Warrington are equally enterprising, and will not be satisfied with anything but the best. Therefore, it was not -Surprising when on the occasion of the annual trip on Thursday, the 700 women who invaded Blackpool enjoyed themselves to the full. It was a day to be remembered by all for, in addition to the arrangements being supremely complete, the Clerk of the Weather was very kind and the sun shone with all its brilliance all day. It was a happy party which left Bank Quay Station in a special train about 8.30 a.m.,

===== 0002601_19140801_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SECOND LARGEST

ASSOCIATION.

Miss CROSFIELD, who was accorded a hearty welcome, said they were the second largest Association in the whole of the Women's Liberal Federation, and it was due in a large measure to Mrs. Osborne and her energetic band of workers that that was so. (Hear, hear.)- In the past they had not had a home of their own, but, thanks to Mrs. John Crosfield, they had now their own rooms, and that being so they must make good use of them. (Hear, hear.) She urged them all to cultivate sympathy with, and understanding of, the work they were out to do. There were people who said that the politics of the Liberal women consisted largely of 'tea fights and enjoying themselves. Tea fights were a good thing; if she had to choose between tea fights, cards and billiards, she thought that lea fights would take the biscuit. (Applause.) They were hoping to have in the winter a series of meetings in the Memorial Hall. The first meet- ing would take place on October 15th, and she would ask them all to attend and listen to the address which would be given by Mrs. Alderton on "Current politics." They should all attend and give them a good send-oil at the first meet- ing in the Memorial Hall. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140801_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD ON THE NEED FOR CLEAR SKIES.

Mr. CROSFIELD had quite an ovation on rising to address the company. Having thanked them for their splendid welcome, he said he agreed ' entirely with what his wife and sister had said in regard to that noble hall in Warrington, and he hoped it would be a source of retreation to the women, and that they would enjoy good acting and good music there. He was also sure that they would get intelligent pleasure out of the instruction they would receive on the affairs of the day from those who came to visit them, like Mrs. Alderton, who, thanks to his sister, would address the first meeting. He could assure them he was full of hope—(applause)— when he thought of all that noble hall would mean in the way of education and rational cul- ture in the future, for their generation, and goodness only knew how many more generations afterwards. (Hear, hear.) They could all sincerely congratulate Mrs. John Crosfield on the choice she made for a memorial to his dear father and the way in which she had had that idea carried out. Applause.) He only wished all skies were as clear as the one they saw in Blackpool. (Hear, hear.) He was optimist enough to refuse to believe till he saw it that the threatened war in Europe would take place. He thought it was positively as- tounding that the "Times" newspaper should immediately assume that whatever might be Russia's action we were bound to fight Russia's ' battle. He did not want to see Old England at daggers drawn with Russia or any other great Power; but in these questions of the Middle East they had got to keep their weather eye open when they were dealing diplomatically with the enormous power of Russia. (Hear, hear.) . .

After making a reference to the tanners' strike, which we report fully under the strike news, Mr. Crosfield spoke on the Irish question and said that there the skies were overcast. , Many of their Liberal friends in Warrington were saying, "Why does the Government not put its foot down?" He could tell them the reason. The Government had been lenient and tolerant to a fault, for the reason that they wanted Ireland, when she started to paddle her own canoe, to do it in smooth waters. (Ap- .plause.) That was the reason they had been patient, tolerant, and lenient, but, pray heaven their Tory friends did not misunderstand them. If the Tories thought that because they had shown that patience they were hesitating as to whether they should turn back on their policy the Tories would find that they had made a mistake which they would live to rue. (Hear, hear.) The Liberal party had cause to be ex- ceedingly proud of their record.He did not envy their political opponents their record or the game they were playing in their desperation, nor attempts they were making to foment insur- rection and disorder in Old Ireland. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

HALL NOOK ESTATE.

Valuable Penketh_Properties in

the Market.

An important sale of valuable freehold farms, building land, ground rents, freehold and lease- hold dwelling-houses, slibps and warehouses at Penketh and Great Sankey, known as the Hall Nook Estate, came under the hammer at the Blue Bell Inn, Horsemarket-street, on Wednes- day, when A. H. Clive and Co., in conjunction with Mr. R. Lowe, auctioneers, offered the vari- ous lots for sale. There was a good attendance. The chief property was the attractive freehold country residence known as Hall Nook, formerly the residence of the late owner, Mr. Robert Garnett, but there was no bidding. The two fields adjoining listed as lots 2 and 3 were not offered. Several bids were made for "Brook- side Farm," "Ash Villa," "Ash Cottage," en bloc and for Ash Cottage separately, but only the latter was sold for £450. For the two semi- detached dwelling-houses in Brook:road. n i Pen- keth, there was some bidding, but ansufficient amount was offered. The reserve was declared at £750. "Brookside Cottage," along with two semi-detached cottages in Chapel-road, Penketh, were withdrawn, although the reserve price of the two cottages was declared at £2BO. The bidding did not reach the required amount for "Vine House," as also was the case in the bid- ding for the three fields formerly part of Brook- house Farm, Station-road. A cottage and gar- den at the corner of Chapel-road and Station- road was sold for .277. Two cottages and gar- dens in Chapel-road were sold at £l6O. The freehold ground rent of £6 13s. 4d. per annum secured on residence of "Brierfield," was disposed of at £135, whilst a freehold ground rent of .68 15s. sd. secured by premises in the occupation of the Union Bank, Man- chester, at Penketh, realised £2OO. For the freehold ground rent of £l2 18s., secured by premises of the Penketh Leather Manufacturing Company, a sum of £260 was realised. The remainder of the lots, including the pro- perties of " Riversdale," "Elm Dene," and the leasehold semi-detached dwelling-house adjoin- ing Elm Done, together with freehold lock-up shop and two warehouses in Dallam-lane, two leasehold dwelling-houses and shops, 87 and 89. Buttermarket-street and corner of Orford-street were withdrawn, no adequate bids being tend- ered. The solicitors for the Hall Nook Estate sale were Messrs. Robert Davies and Co.

RUNCORN GUARDIANS.

There was not much business of importance at this Board on Wednesday. Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presided. _

„ Mrs. Seeker, of Daresbury, wrote intimating her desire to give the children of the Dutton Children's Home an afternoon treat some time during the school holidays. It was proposed to provide tea and games, etc., at Mr. Wade's meadow.

The offer was accepted with thanks. The House Committee reported that an out- break of diarrhcea had occurred among the in- mates and it was suggested that the potatoes being used were the cause.. The Committee in- spected samples raw and boiled, and came to the conclusion they were of good quality, and instructed the Master to continue their use. _

The Board disagreed ;vitiViilW;urg7f,,;tiO-Wihat the potatoes were the cause of the diarrhea, the Chairman saying he had never heard of such a thing.

144 K HOLIDAY SPORTS.

son of the late Mr. William C. Jones, of Leigh, and Mrs. Lawrence, of Llannerch Park, St. Asaph. The ceremony was conducted by the Lord Bishop of Chester, assisted by the Revs. J. Collins and H. V. Pigot. The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in a beautiful dress of white satin, with net tunic 'embroidered in silver and dia- monds. She carried a sheaf of lilies and white heather. The bridesmaids were Misses Joan Greenall (sister of the bride), Marian Logan Wright (cousin of the bridegroom), Misses Cartland, Watkjn and Grendling, and the train-bearers, Misses Helen Spencer, Margaret Davies and Daphne Shepherd-Cross. The bridesmaids' dresses were of white taffeta, with white lace collar capes, pink sashes, and pink aeroplane sailor hats. They carried sheafs of pink gladiolas, and wore diamond arrows, the gifts of the bridegroom. The duties of best man were discharges! by Mr. Talbot Forword. After a reception had been held at Hill Cliffe, Mr. and Mrs. Piers Jones left for London, en route for Deanville, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was of natier blue.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

RE-OPENING OF TIIE ROYAL

COURT.

Bank-Holiday Attraction.

There will be a grand re-opening of the Royal Court Theatre on August Bank Holiday with a matinee at 2.30 and evening performances at 6.50 and 9. Mr. J. A. Campbell's new drama of adventure, "Devil May-Care," a really good play, which is beautifully staged, will be the attraction, and it will doubtless draw large houses.

Advantage has been taken of the recent brief recess to make provision for the additional com- fort of the patrons of the Royal Court. The whole of the building has been re-ventilated, so that the temperature has been very materially lowered, and the pit has been re-seated.

GRAPPENHAL L.

A PEREMPTORY DENIAND.

At the meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Wednesday, Mr. C. E. Linaker pre- siding, the Sanitary Inspector stated that he had received a peremptory demand from a resi- dent in Grappenhall to empty her ashbin. He asked for the Board's instructions. , The Clerk explained that a parish meeting should be called and a resolution passed request- ing the District Council to undertake the empty- ing of ashbins and the removal of nightsoil. The Council could then act. It would be a charge upon the parish. Several other charges would come upon the parish in the immediate future. The Chairman said a special parish meeting should be called. It was not for the Council to 1

move. Mr. Lister-Kaye: What is the use of calling a special meeting? The business would be over in half a minute. Mr. Woodward proposed that the request be accepted to on an undertaking from Mr. Lister- Kaye on behalf of the parish that it was neces- sary, and also accepting full responsibility. Mr. Pilling would not take ally such respon- sibility for his parish. The work, he said, could be done privately, and it was wrong to put it on the rates. - Mr. Lister-Kaye said he would prefer that a parish meeting be called, a suggestion that was accepted, Mr. Woodward's proposition being withdrawn.

' 2. Wastage in electricity production in Dub- lin enormous. Wastage in Belfast nil. The Lord Mayor "This is ludicrous. I challenge anybody to instance a case anywhere of electricity manufacture without waste. It is utterly impossible." 3.—As an instance of jobbery it was stated that a piece of land 50 feet by 150 feet, was sold by the Corporation to an institution for £lO. A member of the deputation asked if there was not some special circumstance in the case to account for the land being practi- cally given away. The answer was an unquali- fied negative. The Lord Mayor: "The piece of land was sold to a charitable institution caring for poor children as a playground. It adjoined the insti- tution, and was of no use for any other pur- pose." We give these points in detail, as they suggest unmistakably that the Unionist Car Sis against the Nationalists must be weak if they have to rely on such instances to support their charges of corruption. Dublin is an old city, with a heritage of debt, and the effects of mismanage- ment in pre-Nationalist days. In spite of this, it has built artisans' dwellings and lodging- houses, public baths and abattoirs, new city markets and' libraries,, has installed a large electricity supply plant, provided an efficient water supply, completed a great scheme of main drainage, and has spent over £250,000 in the clearance of insanitary areas. Much was made of a comparison of the high rates of Dublin with, the relatively low rates of Belfast. This proves nothing. Tile same disparity exists, everywhere, and it is obvious th. in old cities, where there is excessive pov- erty, the rates are bound to be higher than in comparatively new cities, in which there is a considerable amount of highly assessed pro-. perty, great factories, etc. Look at these in- stances:—
