THE RELIGIOUS PERSECUTION MYTH.

From Dublin ;e proceeded to .Mullingar, a town of from 4,700 to 5,000 population, 300 only of whom are Protestants. We were received here by a Mr. Hyde, a Unionist Protestant and Churchman, held in the highest respect in the neighbourhood. He invited questions, and those put to him were mainly on the subject of religion.

One question was: "Is there any evidence of persecution or intolerance on the part of the Roman Catholic majority towards the Protes- tants in this district?" The answer was: "None whatever."

We called on Mr. Lee, a Wesleyan, and put the same question. He replied: "It" (the charge of religious intolerance) "is quite un- true. You may take it from me that there is nothing whatever in the way of religious perse- cution or intolerance in this district." The same assurance was given by Mr. Hutch- inson, jun., of Greville-street, also a Wesleyan, who said that their relations with the Roman Catholics were entirely amicable, and that no ill-feeling of any kind existed.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. HAROLD SMITH'S FUND.

How the Excursion Office Is Run from Warrington.

PAYING THE "EXES."

"Sub Rosa" of the " Daily News and Leader" last week threw some invaluable light upon

the methods by which the" conversions" to

Home Rule are being brought about. After referring to what some of the deputations say, the writer refers to some facts, and hints at what the deputations might say. He con- tinues:—

Rut only a very mean-spirited fellow would say anything of that sort after having been handsomely treated by good, kind Unionist hosts. I have before me an interesting printed letter cm this -ee trips in Ireland, signed r. riarold Smith, the member for Warrington, dated " House of Commons, May 5, 1914." The word "private" at the top of this human document has been crossed out, and as the letter deals with a matter of public interest, and I am sure the author of the letter need not be ashamed of its contents, I will quote some passages from it. This is bow it begins:— My Dear Sir,—You have doubtless beard of the very successful deputations to Ireland which we have organised from my consti- tuency—Warrington, Lancs. In each case we have without difficulty converted every Liberal to the view that Home Rule would be a crime. These have been real conversions indeed. All the Liberals have been persuaded not merely that on the whole the Unionist policy is better than the other, but that Home Rule is wicked and criminal. And this has been done in each case "without difficulty." At the time when the letter was written there had been no failures at all. The success had been complete, and the conversions had been absolute. Next we are introduced to some of the methods employed for spreading the light:— _ . _ _

So successful have these visits proved that I I have had placed at my disposal a sum of money which enables me to offer to selected constituencies facilities for a deputation of five Liberals accompanied by one of our officers for a week's trip in Ireland. Some may think that the phrase "accompanied by one of our officers" rather suggests that the five visitors would be under arrest, but I am Sure nothing of the sort is intended. Indeed, I believe that each of these guides, philosophers, and friends, who accompany the Liberals from selected constituencies, is a helpful and cheery man of the world, who makes the trip "a per- petual feast of nectar'd sweets, where no crude ' surfeit reigns." And now for another very important and at- I tractive part of the scheme:— You would be asked only to send your deputation to Holyhead; paying their fares thereto, and we would pay all the expenses from Holyhead until the members reach their own homes with an allowance up to ...t2 per head to cover loss of wages. There is nothing mean or niggardly about this. I can imagine some men being better off at the end of one of these trips than at the end of an ordinary working week. Of course, the man who has been hospitably entertained for a week in Ireland, and safely delivered at his own home carriage paid, with two pounds in his pocket, may, if he sees fit, denounce the Unionist policy and eulogise Home Rule. There are no condi- tions laid down in this letter—but it would be rather a scurvy trick not to sign a nice official report to the effect that Home Rule would be a mistake—and when the couple of sovereigns slip into the pocket a man may well cross out " mis- take" and substitute "crime."

There is next quite a tantalising hint of even better things in store for the free tripper, as the letter is continued in this way:— This is a mere outline of our proposals and it will be amplified on receipt of a tele- gram from you to the effect that you can immediately arrange such a trip. I wonder what other treats are in store for these favourites of fortune. Perhaps they were allowed to see Sir Edward Carson inspecting a guard of honour, or his Galloper galloping, or they may have heard Captain Craig singing about his help in ages past and his hope for years to come. The nice friendly letter ends in this way : I hope we shall have your co-operation and am sure that most beneficial results may be expected. Perhaps you will consult your member or candidate, as the case may be. After reading this letter, and without know- ing all that is meant by the "amplified pro- posals," I am not surprised to bear of conver- sions among those who are chosen in selected constituencies, after consultation with the mem- ber or candidate, as the case may be.

S. L. H.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE REBEL TRIPPERS. The Majority from Croydon Expose Tory Humbug

AND CHAMPION HOME RULE.

An Irish tour, from July sth to 11th, from Croydon ended disastrously for those who paid the expenses—a matter of doubt, for those who enjoyed the hospitality cannot go further than say "WS understand " they were paid by a Croydon gentleman. Five of the seven members have written and signed a majority report, which states:— We cordially acknowledge the excellence of the arrangements made for our comfort and the generous hospitality extended to us at every place we visited. The itinerary and the general programme were in the hands of Unionists, whose object was to present us with a convincing case from their point Of view. Of this we do not complain, as we were repeatedly invited to make whatever inquiries we chose.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TORY SLANDER UPON DUBLIN.

We first visited Dublin, and were shown the slums of the city. These are deplorable, and we found nobody to condone them—whether Nationalist or Unionist. At luncheon our hosts pointed the moral—the fact that Dublin, under a Nationalist Corporation, is disfigured by wretched slums is proof positive, they say, that the Irish are unfit to govern themselves. This seems to us to be quite unconvincing. On the same line of reasoning the English are unfit to rule, as in all the great cities and many of the smaller towns slums are to be found, in many cases of the worst kind; and it is com- puted that there are some five millions of people living in slum areas. We were left to suppose that the Dublin Corporation was supine and indifferent in the platter, but on inquiry in other directions we found that the Nationalists have expended X 350,000 in building workmen's dwellings, that the present Corporation is car- rying through a big housing scheme to cost over X 116,000, and that it has 15 other schemes in hand. "We have built," said the Lord Mayor, "during 20 years, more houses than any city in Europe. This was substantiated on oath in the course of the Housing Inquiry." Here are three examples of statements made to us at the hotel, and the comments made on them by the Lord Mayor :- 1. Cost of production of electric light in Bel- fast id. per unit. Cost in Dublin 2id. per unit. The Lord Mayor: "The cost of production in Dublin is well under Id. per unit. In Belfast PROVISIONS ARE DEARER.—Advt.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
WILL FIGHT FOR LIBERTY.

Mr. Dowdell, the Chairman of the Nationalist County Council of Westmeath, received the deputation at the County Hall, answered vari- ous questions put to him, and stated the Home Rule case.

One of the questions asked was: "Will the Nationalist Volunteers fight? If so, whom are they prepared to fight, and what for?" This was answered by Councillor Ryan, President of the local section of the Nationalist Volunteer Force. "We will fight," he said, "anyone any- where who attacks the liberties of our country." lie added, 85 per cent. of the people of the country are in favour of Home Rule, and that is a majority you have not got on any question in England."

A great object-lesson in boycotting was the next item in the programme. It would seem that cases of boycotting are few and far be- tween, for this necessitated a journey of 400 miles from Dublin, out and back. It was the famous Kingston case, which seems to be used as a stock example by the Unionists, Mr. Kings- ton having appeared on anti-Home Rule plat- forms in England A certain amount of boycotting is admitted, but as far as the result of our inquiries goes it is entirely a question of "land grabbing," as in the case described. Our last two days were spent in Ulster. At Portadown we were shown over some weaving mills, where work seemed to be carried on under fair conditions. We were shown wages books which indicated that quite good wages were earned. But this, we imagine, must be an exceptional mill, as we were given figures in Belfast, which showed that the average wages in Portadown in the linen industry, taking men and women together, are from 11s. to 13s.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ULSTER VOLUNTEERS.

The Chairman of the District Council and the Town Clerk gave us facts to show the up- to-dateness and excellence of municipal manage- ment of the town.

It was here that we first came into contact with the Ulster Volunteers, a company of which was being drilled. The men, it is evident from our inquiries, take the matter quite seriously. They will not have Home Rule, they say. They object to being placed under the rule of an Irish Parliament, as they are convinced it would mean ruin to the industries of the North, and that they would suffer by increased taxa- tion. We gathered, however, that they did not anticipate that there would be any fighting, al- though some of the men were keen for it.

5

showed that the average wages of all the women was its. 10d., and the average of all the men 15s. 10d. At Lurgan are the best linen-weavers in the world. They had a strike, and it was proved beyond a doubt that the average of the greater number was 12s. a week. They call it unskilled labour. On the contrary, it is highly skilled. At the present time, in one of the largest mills in the city, men are working on a NIGHT SHIFT for 14s. Id. a week. Nine shillings a week at the present time would be an outside average for Belfast women. In short time they earn as little as 3s. or 4s. a week."

_ Wages dockets were handed to us showing that a croel system of fines is in force—de- ducted on the ground of the work not being up to the mark. Here are two instances : "Ss. fine deducted off 14s. id.; 3s 3d. off 12s. These de- ductions are, of course, illegal, and Miss Gal- way's organisation has taken proceedings in some cases, but the workmen are very reluctant to go into court. Facts such as these throw a very ugly light on some of the great industries of Belfast, of which the Unionists are so proud. They talk of their threatened industries. Sams of them were better not threatened but killed outright if they live by such exploitation of the helpless as this. By way of a wind-up of the tour the guests were banqueted and toasted amidst much per- fervid oratory. "Converts" made their confes- sions, and songs in praise of Ulster were sung: The final impression was the distinctly unpleas- ant one left biy a gentleman in the chair who alluded to his fellow countrymen of the South as our "hereditary foes," and talked of the streets of Ulster "running with blood."

===== 0002601_19140801_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
BELFAST POVERTY NOT PARADED.

Belfast was the climax of the tour. We were shown over the great shipbuilding yards of Messrs. Workman and Clark. Protestants and ' Roman Catholics appear to be working amicably together. The incident of two years ago when Catholic workmen were chased out of the works with nuts and bolts was referred to. A state- ment made by one of the Protestant workmen ' in reply to an inquiry suggested that both this incident and the attack on Protestant Sunday school children, which is said to have provoked it, were not in any real sense religious in origin, but they were pure hooliganism, and were con- fined to a few young and turbulent spirits. We questioned one of our guides as to why these works were visited by the deputations in- stead of the larger works of Messrs. Harland and Wolff, the builders of the great White Star liners, the head of which firm is a Home Ruler? The reply was that many deputations had been taken to the latter works, and there was an impression that it had been over-done.' We were shown round the excellently equipped Technical Institute, the scope of which was ex- plain6d by the Principal, and a reception of the deputation was held by the Lord Mayor. He addressed the members, and afterwards sub- mitted himself to a vigorous heckling from his audience.

Fear for their industries, fear of corrupt gov- ernment, and fear of being unfairly taxed were the main motives of the opposition to Home Rule expressed in the Lord Mayor's remarks. We were then shown the fine buildings, the magnificent Town Hall, the spacious streets, the great works of the city. But this is not all of Belfast. There is plenty of poverty, but it is not deliberately brought under one's notice, as were the slums of Dublin. The Falls-road and Shankhill-road districts, for instance, represent the other side of the picture.

And how many inquire as to the methods by which much of the wealth of Belfast is made? They talk of liberty, they talk of the Empire and the flag, but what about the cruel sweating that disgraces Belfast's great linen industry? The organisers of deputations from England never mention that. But we were out to get in- formation, and we made it our business to call on Miss Galway, the secretary of the Women Textile Workers' Organisation. In the Ulster linen trade, we were told, wages have been con- tinually going down for years. "1906 was a boom year. We never had such a year. The employers' own figures, their own statements. ,

===== 0002601_19140801_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME RULE OVERDUE.

To sum up, we have seen nothing to alter our conviction that the grant of Home Rule is over- due, and we are convinced that it is desired by the great majority of the people of Ireland. The nearly 200,000 Irish Nationalist Volunteers are a sufficient answer to the charge of indifference. Education is slowly but surely making headway in the South. Give the Irish the power to man- age their own affairs and you will create a sense of responsibility and a zeal for the common good thilt will, in time, bring a beneficent change in the social conditions. "Go round to the villages hereabouts," said a farmer in the South, "and you will find a class of young men educated and well-informed on questions of the day." It is there that the hope of the future lies—the new Ireland in which the religions pro- blems will be solved by advancing education. As a sign of the direction in which things are tending we would point to the labourers' cot- tages that have been, and are being, built in various parts of Ireland—the outcome of the efforts of the Nationalist party.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
FEARS GROUNDLESS.

We think the fears of Ulster are groundless. In the first place, religious intolerance is dying out, and the South is becoming daily more en- lightened. Secondly, it is utterly incredible that the Irish Parliament should do anything to cripple the great industries of the North or Impede the development of the virile and enter- prising province of Ulster.

Rather will the South, when the present per- plexing problems are out of the way and she is no longer obsessed with ancient grievances, seek to emulate her Northern neighbour, to foster industry in her urban centres, and to werk per- sistently for the revival of agriculture. We consider that the Amending Bill is a generous concession, and should remove the Ulster grievance, but we are in favour of the removal of the time limit if this will facili- tate a settlement, as we are convinced that in a few years the Ulster counties will see that it is to their advantage to come into the scheme. In any case, we are of opinion that Ulster has not a shred of justification for iirned rebellion against the constituted Governte:,- . of the king- dom.

(Signed)

Presentation by Local Conservatives.

A HANDSOME GIFTOFSILVZL

At St. Margaret's Church, Westminster, on Wednesday, Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., was mar- ried to Miss Joan Furneaux, youngest daughter of the late Rev. Henry Furneaux, of Oxford, who is sister to the wife of Mr. F. E Smith, K.C., M.P., the bridegroom's brother. At the church the Dean of Wind ester and Canon Willis, rector of Warrington, officiated. The bride was attired in a gown of soft white satin charmeuse, draped with Iranerick lace. aLt from the shoulders 'ell a (curt train of silver and white brocade, lined With silver tissue and gathered tulle. Her lace veil, which was ovcr another of tulle, was arranged with a dia- mond slide on the hair and circlet of orange fpwcrs. There were six bridesmaids and three little pages in attendance. The six brides- maids wore pearl brooches given by the bride- groom._

A SCENE OF FLAG WAVING.

Close upon 5,000 people, mostly ladies and children, gathered on the Warrington Football Ground at Wilderspool on Saturday afternoon to witness the presentation of about .t. 500 worth of silver and glass ware as a present to Mr. , Harold Smith on the occasion of his marriage, which took place on Wednesday, as narrated above. _ _ _ _

--•-• PRINCIPALS ARRIVE. At a few minutes to four o'clock it wag whis- pered on the platform that the company from Walton Hall had arrived. A cheer from the fringe of ,the crowd announced that the party was approaching. Then the flags began to wave and the crowd were led on to cheer the more. This continued until the platform in front of the grand stand was reached.

Miss Furneaux was, of course, the cynosure of all eyes, and she seemed deeply touched by the welcome. Sli:o wore a black toque ',trimmed with white plumes, a black silk flounced dress, around the waist of which was a wide sash of blue ribbon tied in a large bow behind. Mr. Fulton Smith, in +the absence of Mr. Peter Itylands, presided. Sir Gilbert Greenell made the presentation on behalf of the War- rington Conservative Association. They had watched, said Sir Gilbert, Mr. Smith's career - with admiration, and hoped he would long con- tinue as their member in Warrington. Lady Greensll made a presentation on behalf of the Women's Unionist and Tariff Reform League. All wished the prospective married couple the greatest of happiness. MR. SMITH'S REPLY. Mr. Harold Smith expressed his deepest thanks to his friends in • Warrington who had macW., Miss Furneaux and himself such handsome presents. Ire could not adequately express his feelings over their kindness. ANGLING.

W arrington Workingmen's Associa-

N.A. CENTRE.

THE OPENING MATCH.

The opening match at Aintree was a success all along, both in sport and entries. We are most pleased to report that the entry is the . largest ever recorded in any of our opening matches; sixty-eight fished the match and forty- seven caught fish. This alone will speak for the canal as an ideal spot for the contest angler, especially when we consider the awful weather we have had to contend with. As we are all aware, the Bay Horse Club had their contest when it simply poured all day, and on Sunday last it was blowing almost a gale during the two hours the members were engaged in their match, and there must have been a hundred and one bites not seen by the most experienced angler. If that was so, how did the inexperienced novice fare? There were more novices at this match than we have noticed at any other contest. Whether they believe they have a better chance in fishing a canal than a river we are not prepared to say, but it goes without argument that it is more I equal to all concerned to fish in a canal, as a more even current is obtained, and everyone has the same depth of water to fish in. Besides, there are no favourite holes that certain classes of fish harbour in.

In the list below names will be found that have not appeared since the days of the Acton Bridge contests. It is only right and just that these members should have a turn; they are more at home in a canal than a river, and members who have been always well up in the prize list cannot grumble at the turn of for- tune's wheel. The winner was found in H. Moores, who is ! well known as an experienced paste angler, and he informs us that in the contest he used neither feeder nor maggot. We do not think there was another member in the match that adopted these methods, the remainder of the cracks pin- ning their faith to maggot fishing, which has served them well in the past.

ITEMS. The distribution of prizes will be reported next week. We notice that the first three in the Bay Horse match are well down the list in the Centre match. Members say it is far better to travel to matches by saloon than in ordinary carriages. Will the Northern Anglers of Warrington ever get an ideal day for match fishing? our annual match break all records?

According to the amount of prizes we should say so. When are we going to have a decent fish from our canals?

- Who said these holidays?

RESULT OF CONTEST AT AINTREE.

TERRITORIAL PARADE.

Rev. W. Bracecamp and the Trend of the Age.

The annual church parade of the South Lan- cashire Regiment of the 4th battalion, Prince of Wales' Volunteers, took place on Sunday morn- ing. There are more than 700 men in the regi- ment. and as only 120 of these, including the band and drums, attended the parade, it proved numerically disappointing. Prior to the march- ing off from the Drill Hall to St. Paul's Church long-service medals- were presented to Sergeants Rossward and Whitfield and Private Nolan by Lieutenant-Colonel B. Fairclougb, who con- gratulated and shook hands with the recipients.

Colonel Fairclough commanded the Terri- torials on parade, who were accompanied by a detachment of the Army Service Corps. For these several of the front seats in the church were reserved, the band occupying a prominent position, as it accompanied "Onward, Christian Soldiers," "Fight the Good Fight," and other suitable hymns.

The Chaplain's Sernioni

The Rev. W. Bracecamp, chaplain to the regi- ment, conducted the service and preached the sermon, which he commenced by drawing a contrast between that moLing's attendance and the attendance which would he seen in the Ifornby Church in a fortnight, when the soldiers were camping. One felt sad and humiliated when one remembered that this was the most important Territorial parade of the year in Warrington.

The soldiers had to qualify themselves to go into camp by performing certain drills, all of which were important; but from his point of view, this parade was the most important, be- cause their success depended upon the Giver of all good things, and they could not hope to be successful if they did not recognise the King of kings. He did not wish to blame those present, but he would like them to feel so disgusted that they would .induce the absentees in future to take an interest in the church parade. It was a pity that the soldiers were not compelled to attend the Warrington service, as they had to at camp. If it had been a football or boAing match the building would not have been large enough to accommodate those who would have desired to attend. That was exactly the trend of the age—an age of pleasure, excitement, and selfishness, in which God came last in every- thing. It was not an easy task, in our daily lives, to conform to Christ's teaching; it cost something to do that. We should be valiant and mighty, loyal and true to those over us, especially to the King of kings. Thus we should be the true type of soldier.

_ When the South African War was in pro- gress, some men in a London tavern were criti- cising a certain general in a manner not particu- larly nice. In one corner of the tavern a man stood up at length and rebuked them severely, saying that he had served under the general in question. There was an example of perfect loy- alty. If we were loyal to one another, to our superiors, to the King as subjects, and to the King of kings it would be better for us as indi- viduals, and better for the nation at large. The band concluded the service with an ex- pressive voluntary.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 1, 1914.

ALTRINCHAM LIBERALS.

Addressed by Sir John Simon at

Belle Vue.

UNIONISTS AND ULSTER.

The Liberals of the Altrincham Division met at Belle Vue on Saturday for the double purpose of hearing Sir John Simon and of meeting Mr. Edward Powell, the recently chosen Liberal candidate for the division, who followed the Attorney-General wall a brief speech. Mr. J. A Morris, one of the staunchest of Lancashire and Cheshire Liberals, presided. Sir John Simon left large tracts of the Government's intentions unexplored, but, as something to be going on with, he made three welcome reassurances. He repeated Mr. Asquith's declaration that the King's part in the Conference had been strictly according to the understanding of the Constitution, which limits public acts of the King to those which his Ministers advise. "It is on the advice of his Ministers," Sir John said, "and subject to his Ministers being censured if they do wrong, that the Royal action is always taken." The other assurances were that Home Rule is without doubt to be placed on the Statute Book, and that there will not be a General Election until Home Rule, Welsh Disestablishment, and the Plural Voting Bill are the law of the hina. The allusion to plural voting was cheered in- cisively.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ULSTER CONFERENCE.

Sir John Simon had some personal opinions to express about the Conference which were int-AroAtiog. Although they did not lift so much as the fringe of the curtain which hid away its sittings. He sees in the meeting round the same table of representatives of the different Irish parties to discuss Irish affairs the first sign of the beginning of the management of Ireland's business by Irishmen, and he revealed a per- sonal hope that the conversations have done something to show each side in this quarrel that perhaps there is a means of reconciliation and adjustment." His idea seems to be that when other resources fail the irreconcilables may turn back to this Conference for threads that may be woven into terms of accommoda- tion. But he thinks that one or two mistaken notions in the minds of Unionists in Ulster and Great Britain must first be knocked on the head. It is an illusion that threatened violence will upset the orderly progress of legislative enactment, and it is equally vain to rely on son ething happening to bring about a General Election before Home Rule is law.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD TEMPER, FIRM CONVICTION.

I have come this afternoon, said Sir John Simon, to bring you a message of good cheer; to say that those who serve under the Prime Min- ister—(cheers)—were never more devoted to his wise leadership. What we need now is to show good temper, firm conviction, and a determina- tion to press on, being very certain that no party in the State ever received or ever deserved to receive the support of Englishmen and Eng- lishwomen unless it showed that, while-it was anxious to make friends, the mere fact that it was resisted was no reason why it should give up principles for which it had fought. (Cheers.) Mr. Edward Powell, the prospective Liberal candidate for the Altrincham Division, said that Liberals were resolved that the fear of rebellion should not rob them of the advantages of the Parliament Act. The rflessaige whichtSir John Simon had brought had cheered their hearts. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19140801_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
UNABATED CONFIDENCE.

On the motion of Mr. W. E. Thompson, sec- onded by Mr. Frank Milne, the following reso- lution was unanimously adopted:—

"This meeting expresses its unabated confi- dence in the Prime Minister; assures him of its full endorsement of his Home Rule policy, and pledges itself to support him in his resolve not ta be forced into a General Election until the full programme under the Parliament Act las been achieved."

===== 0002601_19140801_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE COURTS.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

MONDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Bolton, Alderman Smethurst, Alderman James Evans, and Messrs. S. Wright, W. Furness,

- - and G. Pilling. • Not Worked for Five Years.—David Poulton, no fixed abode, when taken in charge by Detec- stive-Setgeant Holland for begging stated that he had not worked for five years, and did not at all believe in the Insurance Act. (Laughter.) The Mayor: Have you worked within the last SAX months?—Poulton : Yes; at a little garden- ing.—The Chief Constable: A great deal of his gardening has been done by the roadside. (Laughter.)—He was sent to prison for 14 days. Police Assault—James Roach, no fixed abode, was charged with refusing to quit the Old House at Home public-house, Barbauld-street, on Sun- day night and with assaulting P.C. Harrison. Mr. Mullins, the licensee, said that the man had had sufficient drink when he entered the house and was refused. He declined to go out, and P.C. Harrison was called. The latter officer said when he was getting Roach out he kicked him about the legs and body.—Fined 10s. and costs with the option of 14 days.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
THURSDAY.

Before Messrs. J. Rymer Young (in the chair), C. S. Burgess, W. Roberts, A. J. Jolley and Dr. Naden.

A Street in Tumult.—Catherine Clancy, Dial- street, was charged with being disorderly in Dial-street.—A. constable said that there was a crowd of about 300 persons present. The street was in a tumult.—The Chief Constable said that if such a state continued in Dial-street it would be necessary to have constables permanently stationed there.—The woman was fined 20s. and costs, with the option of one month.—Accused : I will go to prison for the mouth; nobody is going to pay for rue. The Seven 'Rabbits.—Thomas 'O'Brien, Quay Fold, was charged with having been found in possession of game.—A constable said he found him with seven rabbits, six of which were alive. As soon as he approached defendant he, ran away.—O'Brien explained that on Sunday-morn- ing he was walking near the Twelve Arches, when he found the rabbits in a bag under some hay. He was not a poacher.—The Chairman thought it was unusual for a man to find rabbits in a bag, but as there wa?nothing against the man they would impose a fine of 55., including costs.

STOCKTON HEATH

HIGH SCHOOL PRIZE DAY.

Mrs. 11. Greenall and Bygone

Education.

The annual prize distribution, presentation of certificates, and exhibition of work in con- nection with Miss Partington's high school was hekl on Wednesday afternoon. A large number of parents and friends of the scholars assembled in the Hall Cafe, Victoria-square, for the first part of the proceedings, over which Miss Part- ington presided.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
HEAD MISTRESS'S REPORT.

In presenting her annual report the head mistress stated that during the past year the number of scholars in the school had increased , from 60 to 79. (Applause.) The Kindergarten class was making splendid progress. There had not been quite the usual number of musical successes, owing to a change in the staff, but: more were expected next year. In the 1913. Oxford local examinations three scholars passed in the senior and nine in the preliminary divi- sion. (Applause.) In the London matricula- tion in English language Mademoiselle Germaine Roussell was successful, and Ethel Mclntyre passed in Grade 111, of the Incorporated Society of Musicians last December. In the examina- tion held by that Society this month Doris Butterworth and Ethel Mclntyre passed in Grade IV. (advanced), the former gaining a dis- tinction. Doris Gunner obtained second class honours in the Royal Drawing Exhibition held this year in London, whilst in the Royal Draw- ing Society examinations four passed in Divi- sion 111. (two with honours), and five of the seven who passed in Division 11. gained honours. In the first division all the seven who passed were awarded honours, and the same result attended the efforts of the seven who passed in the preparatory division. Satisfactory progress was being made in the school games, though they still lacked a hockey field. She had much pleasure in calling upon Mrs. H. Greenall to present the prizes.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRIZE LIST.

- Mrs. Greenall then gracefully presented prizes to the following : English.—V.: Beatrice Traropleasure (given by Mrs. Bower) ; IV.: Marjorie Bell; III.: Madge Halliwell; II.: Dorothy Gandy. Arithmetic.—V.: Dorothy Griffiths (given by Mr. James Young); IV.: Kathleen Pimlott (given by Mr. James Young); III.: Doris Hor- ton (upper) and Kathleen Geddes (lower); II.: Stella Johnson.

French.—V.: Dorothy Griffiths (given by Dr. Bower); IV.: Lallie Newcombe; III.: Dorothy Bennett; IL: lolene Brady. Latin.—Matriculation Class: Sybil Plant (given by Miss Broadbent); V.: Mary Mclntyre; IV.: Eileen Bell. sclence.,--11pper : Dorothy Halliwell; Middle: Rtth Griffiths. Sew ing.—Lizzie Frodsham. Music.—Doris Butterworth (given by Mrs. A. C. Lerma). ' Drawing.—Doris Gunner (given by Mrs. Bower). Tennis Championship.—Sybil Plant. • Tennis Tournament—Dorothy Halliwell. Badminton Tournament—Mary Shepherd.

"A GREAT RECORD."

Mrs. Greensll considered that this was a great record. The scholars had done well. Education had, of course changed since the old days, and- it was very hard to gain these successes now that the competition was so keen. All parents should give their children a good education, especially as the lessons were now made so interesting. In her days they were a most fear- ful bore from beginning to end--(laughter)—and even if she gained a prize it would prove to be a dull book. There were never any sports prizes, either.

However, she would advise the children to take, every possible opportunity of improving their knowledge. -.She had no doubt they -did, and they should continue to do so. (Applause.) Handsome bouquets were then presented to Mrs. Greenall .by Marjorie Young, and to Miss Partington 'by Jack Garton.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ENTERTAINMENT.

After Mr. W. Darlington, the visiting pro- fessor at Stockton Lodge, had rendered one of Grieg's sonatas in fine style on the piano the scholars proceeded to give an excellent enter- tainment, consisting of balloon and gavotte dances (in costume); pianoforte selections by Marian Holden, Sybil Plant, Margaret Dowling, Mary Roberts, Marjorie Young, and Miss Hart- ley; and Kindergarten action songs and part songs. "Miss Barker's Tea Party," the well- known scene from " Cranford," was also pre- sented in capital style by the upper girls in appropriate costumes, the parts being as fol- lows: Miss Barker, Sybil Plant ;frs. Forrester, Marian Holden; the Hon. Mrs. Jamieson, Muriel Hall; Miss 3fatty, Mildred Geddes; Miss Pole, Rhona ifamman; Miss Mary Smith, Barbara Johnson; and Peggy, Elsie Booth.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
VOTE OF THANKS.

Mrs. Plant, of Crewe, proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Greenall for digtributing the prizes. She thought the school had made splendid progress, especially in the Oxford examinations. Of course, this high standard could not be main- tained without the co-operation of the parents, which she trusted, as a governess, would be forthcoming. Mrs. Jeans seconded, and the motion, was car- ried with acclamation, Mrs. Greenall suitably replying.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
_EXHIBITION OF WORK.

The visitors then proceeded to Stockton Lodge, in the grounds of which tea was served, after- wards inspecting the numerous specimens of painting, carving, basketry, and needlework. which had been executed by the scholars and were exhibited in one of the schoolrooms.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
PENS ETH.

WEDDING OF MISS Q. SNODDY.

The Wesleyan Chapel was very prettily deco- rated on Thursday afternoon for the wedding ceremony of Miss QUeenie Snoddy, (laughter of Mr. James Smylie Snoddy, of • Cherry Tree House, Penketh, and Mr. Ernest Shaw, of St. Helens. The bride's dress was of white charmeuse, draped bodice opening over a vest of gold- threaded lace. An overskirt of ninon trimmed with Honiton point lace (specially made by the bride's mother), caught up with sprays of orange blossom. She also wore a full court train lined with rucked chiffon, a hem outlined with seed pearls, and trimmed with true lovers' knots of twisted chiffon, intertwined with pearls, sprays of orange blossom, and white heather. Her veil was of Brussels net embroidered with true lovers' knots held by a wreath of orange ' blossom. The shower-bouquet was composed of white roses and lilies of the valley. Touches of gold lace on her dress added originality to a lovely toilette.

The bride's father gave her away. Miss Irene Snoddy, sister of the bride, acted as bridesmaid, dressed in pink satin with tunic overdress of painted ninon looped up with satin ribbon to match, and a leghorn hat trimmed with pink French roses, pale pink chiffon under the brim and black velvet streamers. She carried a shower bouquet of La France roses. The bride's mother wore blue silk with touches of dull gold lace, blue hat with black plumes, and carried a bouquet of pink carnations. The officiating ministers were the Rev. H. Middlewood, of Penketh, and the Rev. S. Morris, of Runcorn. Mr. Alfred Pinnington, of St. Helens; acted as best man.

The reception was held at the bride's home, and the wedding breakfast partaken of in the marquee on the lawn. A large number of tele- grams and congratulations were received during the afternoon. The presents were both costly and numerous. ,

After the wedding the bride and bridegroom left for Whiting Bay, Scotrand, where the honeymoon is ,being spent. • _ .

The bride's .travelling dregs was a tailor-made Navy-blue costume, with a black leghorn hat trimmed with black lace, fruit, and flowers.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
LYMM.

Members Present.—There were present at the Lymm Council meeting on Monday Councillors Warburton (in the chair), Groves, Cowper, Holt, Critchley, Lockyer, Gotobed, Parker and Ingham, with Messrs. W. Mullard (clerk), D. Mort (surveyor), and W. L. Donaldson (gas manager).

Aid for Local Authorities—ln regard to the prospective contribution from the Imperial Exchequer to local authorities, Councillor Groves, giving a report of the Urban District Councils' Association conference at the Council meeting on Monday, said there Was a strong opinion that whatever amount was received should not go towards the relief of rates, but should be used for public improvements. He shared that view. If they in Lymm re- ceived an amount equal to 9d. or 4d. in the X they could easily find some use for it, without reducing the rates.

Local Wedding.—The marriage of Miss Ethel Molyneaux, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Moly- neaux, of Heatley, to Mr. Thomas Elliott, of Lymm, was solemnised on Thursday in the Parish Church, the Rector (Rev. G. B. Thurs- ton) officiating. There was a full choral service. The bride was given away by her father, and was attended by two bridesmaids— her own sister and the sister of the bride- groom. After the ceremony the newly-married couple left for North Wales, where the honey- moon will be spent.

The Fire Brigade.—At Monday's meeting of the Council an account was presented for con- firmation for £22 17s. 9d. for the services of the Fire Brigade at Messrs. Lowe's premises in Cherry-lane. It was suggested by Councillor Parker that the accounts ought to be held over until the proceedings connected with the fire had been gone into. It was objected that complaints ought not to be made unless the captain was present to hear them; and the Clerk explained that the accounts were according to scale.—On the motion of Councillor Groves, the account was passed.

Road Repair.—The Roads Committee of the Council met on July 20th, and in respect to the road opposite Dane Bank it was proposed by Councillor Warburton and seconded by Coun- cillor Groves, that the repair of this road be pro- ceeded with at once.—Councillor Gotobed moved and Councillor Cowper seconded an amend- ment that the repair of the road be not pro- ceeded with.—Councillors Gotobed and Cowper voted for the amendment, and the remaining members voted against it. The resolution was therefore carried.—At the Council meeting on Thursday Councillor Gotobed objected to the wording of the resolution. His amendment was that the work be not proceeded with until they 'were legally entitled to do so. The contract was not complete.—lt was decided to add the fol- lowing words to Councillor Gotobed's amend- ment (which was. carried) "Until the convey- ance be proceeded with."

Hospital Matters.—At a meeting of the Sani- tary Committee of the Lymm 'Urban District Council the clerk was instructed to advertise for a caretaker at the hospital—man and wife, without children, the man to work under the superintendence of the Inspector. The wages for the man are to be £1 Is. per week; wages for caretaker 2s. 6d. per week, but in the event of patients being in the hospital, the wages of the caretaker are to be at the rate of 10s. per week, instead of 2s. 6d., with house, fuel, light, and water. Councillor Cowper voted against the resolution.

At the Council meeting on Monday Councillor Cowper said he was of the opinion that the Council ought to advertise for a certified nurse. People, had been much upset about a recent case, arid, he was 'certain that plenty of women could be found to undertake the duties if an advertisement were issued. Of course, he knew that 'the Council were against him on the point. The Chairman: You know the resolution which was passed.

Councillor Cowper: Cannot we speak here at all? Are we muzzled?

The Chairman: You must first bring it up in committee. We don't want to waste film. Councillor Groves said that if Councillor Cow- per had a grievance about hospital affairs he would be quite within his rights to give notice to rescind the resolution of the committee. Councillor Cowper again asked the Council to include a certified nurse in the advertisement, but the Chairman said the matter was already settled and could not be re-opened without notice.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
FAMILY LIVES ON 7s. A WEEK.

Workroom Used as a Dwelling.

A strange story was told at Sale on Monday, when Robert Leigh, Bridge Foot, Lymm, was charged with-neglecting his five children. It is stated that the man with his wife and family lived in a disused workroom in which were no sanitary conveniences or water. There was one large room. and two small ones, and no rent was paid. ' • Defendant was of drunken habits, but the mother did not drink. The Mother gave birth to a child in May, but it died in July. An in- quest was held, and the verdict was returned to the effect that death was due to starvation caused by insufficient nourishment. • There was only one bed in the house, which was covered with an old sheet. The man gave his wife 7s. a week. He seemed, to have lost all affection through his continual (kinking. The man's defence was that he ,had been handicapped by a big family. He worked for farmers about Lymm, and every night borrowed money to take home. He gave his wife £1 a week. He denied that he was of drunken habits, and said he did not spend money, which was wanted for food, in drink.

A Lymm woman stated that the children had not been short of food, and that prisoner was a considerate father.

Defendant was sent to gaol.for three months, the chairman remarking that acc...ised had given way to drink and was lazy.

SUNDAY FISHING.

While "Big Guns" Golf Should Workers Fish? LIVELY COUNCIL DEBATE.

Fishing to be Allotted.

Should fishing be allowed in the Dam on Sunday mornings until 8 o'clock? • This was a question on which there was a great deal of discussion at the monthly meeting of the, Council on Monday evening. One of the strong arguments for the proposi, tion was that if Sunday golf he allowed for the rich, what harm is there in the working man indulging in his sport in the early hours of Sunday. On the other hand it was contended that such a proposal gave the idea that the Council was " hard Up" for coppers, and it was also stated that it was an undesirable state of affairs.

LETTERS OF PROTEST. The fallowing letters were read by the Clerk. The Rev. E. E. Lovell, Baptist Church, Higher- lane. wrote:— _ _ _

" We have heard with regret that applica- tion has been made to you for permission for Sunday fishing in the Dam. We urge you in what we believe to be the bescinterests.of the

district not to grant the application." The Rev. R. W. Ferguson, superintendent minister of the Primitive Methodist Connexion, also wrote:—

"We have heard with regret of the applica- tion to be made for permission to be allowed to fish in the Dam on Sundays. We caunek think that the granting of such application

will be in the best interests of the—district.

We sincerely trust that a matter of such importance will receive your best considera- tion." COUNCILLOR HOLT'S RESOLUTION.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
Councillor Holt moved the following resolu- tion:—

:inburg,

"That the rules regarding Sunday fishing be rescinded and that fishing be allowed up to 8 o'clock on Sunday morning." • He said that was a recommendation to allow fishing in the Dam on Sunday mornings. He had heard there was some objection to their granting the permission. In the letters, for instance, they were told distinctly there was objection. He had thought the matter out before making that proposition, and he had come to the conclusion that when they stopped the rich man having his game of golf, and when their ministerial friends gave An travelling by train on Sundays, then, and not before then, was such time to tell the poor man not to have an hour or two hours' fishing early on Sundays. Ile objected to the fishing later in the day, and did not want people to see the anglers sitting on the bank when they were going to church or having a walk round, but he did not see any reason why they should not fish until eight o'elock, if they could conscientiously do so.

Councillor Lockyer seconded. "THESE GREAT GUNS."

Councillor Cowper: Here we have these great guns in the village going golfing, and I think the working man has a right to fish up to eight o'clock, but I would not agree to it past that hour. You have got the Dam, which is a nuisance—(laughter)--but still, let them fish if they want to. Councillor Parker : I support the resolution. I think myself, as a working man, they are en- titled to have a bit of fishing on Sunday morn- ings. I would like to draw Mr. Holt's attention to the fact that he did not refer to trippers. I should object to trippers coming in. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Holt: I only mean residents.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
NEITHER FOR NOR AGAINST.

Councillor Gotobed : I am not going to vote against the resolution, and I shall not vote for it--not that I think tbere is any great sin in having a few hours' fishiUg on Sundays—but I am not sure that we, as a public body, are doing the right thing. I think we are setting a bad precedent. I agree with Councillor Holt in what he said with reference to the rich men and golf, but that does not relieve us of a cer- tain obligation. Whether the rich man plays golf or not, that doea not make it right or make it any more desirable that fishing should take place on Sundays. I am a keen fisher myself, but I object to going on Sundays. I have as little time as any man during the week, but I find the time then to enjoy my sport without going on Sundays. I wont go against the resolution, but I cer- tainly won't go for it, because I cannot con- scientiously do so. I don't think we, as a public body, \should support it. Councillor Holt men- tioned something about a requisition to the Council. I have not seen it. CHAIRMAN'S AMENDMENY.,

The Chairman (Councillor Warburton): I am afraid I must put in an amendment. When it comes that a municipal body are going in for municipal trading on a Sunday, it is like a drowning man grasping at a straw. It makes it appear we have got to-get money from some- where and somehow; that we must have it by allowing fishing on Sundays. We should also have to employ labour On Sundays to see that no poachers came to the Dam, employing a man in the time when he should be with his family, or, preparing to go to some place of worship or elsewhere. It is not right nor just, and there is nothing feasible about it. I say it would be a cruel thing on behalf of a few, I don't suppose we have a dozen men who take out fishing tickets in the place. If we go in for catering for these few on Sundays, why not go even further and supply boats for those who prefer boating on Sunday's?

There is as much right in one thing as in the other. Then, again, why should we not have bowling, cricket and football matches on Sun- days? There is no more 'objection to the one than to the other. It would be a most scan- dalous thing for us as a public body. You can take it as you like. It seems to me you can go fishing up to a certain time, pack up your traps and go to church. It looks well, I must say. We cannot stop these people who go— Councillor Lockyer: Golfing! Why do you want to stop-the working man having an hour's fishing?.

_ .The Chairman: There is no one here who will defend the working man more than myself, but I do say that if he has no time other than Sun- day morning it is God help him! and God help us. The question seems to me that for the grasping of a few coppers we are going to allow this. That is not principle. Are we going to do any good by that? If you can do any good by it I am with you, but not unless.

PRIVILEGE FOR THOSE WHO ASK. Councillor Ingham: The idea is to give this privilege to those who ask for it. Councillor Cowper: I don't know why the Chairman talked about municipal trading. TABLE TALK.

The World's Tribute of Interesting Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

Arsenic in certain carpets was found to be the cause of hitherto undiscovered maladies in various cases noted by the German scientist, Dr. Kuttner. It may appear singular that the presence of carpets should be detrimental to the health, but he observed five cases with dis- tinct symptoms of aruemia and the like, and these disappeared when the carpets were re- moved. He surmised from the colour that the carpets might contain arsenic, and when they were analysed this was found to be true, and in a considerable proportion. Traces of arsenic were also found in the bodies of the persons.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
The Value of a Rain-gauge.

The rain-gauge is a very simple instrument. It comprises a cylindrical can with a removable top constructed like a funnel; inside the cylin- der is a smaller can or glass bottle to receive the rain. A graduated glass, marked to hun- dredths of an inch, completes the outfit. The whole may be obtained from any scientific in- strument maker for an outlay of a few shillings. Every farmer, gardener, fruit grower should instal a rain-gauge, and it would be a good thing if one were set up in every school garden; the actual measuring of each day's rainfall could be demonstrated to the children.—" Agri- cultural Economist and Horticultural Re- view."

A Flower Show in Lang Acre.

A flower show in busy, bustling Long Acre, London, is certainly a novelty. With a view to popularising the new stock, Sir Henry Vansit- tart Neale, and the new pea, One and All Sen- sation, the Council of the Agricultural and Hor- ticultural Association are offering liberal money prizes, medals, and books for competition on August 13th. An interesting display of the two novelties is expected. Classes are also included for asters (Ostrich Plume), and onions (One and All Giant). Forms of entry are obtainable from the secretary, 92, Long Acre, London, W.C.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticul- tural Review."

===== 0002601_19140801_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
40,000 Years Old.

A skeleton which was discovered in 1903 in a cave—" the Cheddar man," as it is known to science—and which has been declared by savants in various parts of the world to be from 40,000-to 80,000 years old, was the object of ad- dresses by Professor Seligmann and Professor Parsons before members of the Royal Anthrop- ological Institute. The skull has dimensions at the back of the temple bones not met with in the case of other specimens. The jaw is very powerfully formed, and its width is in excess of that of any modern man. The skeleton is claimed to be the most ancient yet discovered.

Trees and Lightning. Trees rich in starch and deficient in fatty oils offer the least resistance, while those con- taining plenty of fatty substances are rarely struck during a thunderstorm. The lime be- longs to the latter category, but any tree stand- ing alone, as was the lime beneath which the unfortunate children sheltered is liable to be struck, particularly when several people congre- gate together near its trunk. The elm should always be avoided during an electrical storm. Even more dangerous than elm trees are the poplar and oak trees. The safest trees are wal- nut, lime, beech and birch; M. Flammarion' failed to trace a single instance of the latter having been struck by lightning when he col- lected statistics on the subject some time ago.

"Theft of Ethereal Love." The first suit on record in the American ' courts to recover damages for the wrecking of a spiritual marriage has just been dismissed in the Federal Court in West Virginia. The suit wogs brought by Mrs. Ella Thomas, a medium of the Bridgeport (Ohio) Spiritualist Church, forty years old, against Miss Pearl Powell, of Wheeling, for stealing the love of her "spiritual husband,i' Mr. George Heinlein, aged twenty- six. The plaintiff admitted there was no legal wedding. Their spirits were simply united a year and a half ago, and the course of ethereal love ran smoothly till Mr. Beinlein saw Miss Powell last spring, when its course was vio- lently interrupted. The Court held that as all the alleged wrongs were committed in the 1 spiritual world, it had no jurisdiction.

Chinese " Gobelins " Tapestry. The fact that a Chinese factory of "Gobelins" tapestry must have flourished in Pekin during the eighteenth century is now fully established by the recent discovery by Mr. T. J. Larkin during his travels in the East of a large panel of this work, woven on European lines, and prob- ably executed by Chinese workmen under the instruction of French experts. This remarkable panel, which represents a scene of birthday ceremonial within the Palace of the Emperor Kien Lung, appears to have been intended by him as a gift to King Louis XV. of France, for its border is an exact reproduction of that be- longing to the tapestries by Boucher produced at the Gobelins as a gift to China from Frank.

Shellac for Waterproofing. The members of the staff of fount Rose Ob- servatory, Nevada, have found shellac to be an impervious and pliable waterproofing for rub- bers, which otherwise during a day's use on the snowfields absorb sufficient moisture from the snow to dampen the feet. An application of the shellac at infrequent intervals kept the rub- bers in perfect condition. It was also found that a coating of thellac on the thongs of snow- shoes prevented them from becoming quickly water-soaked on wet snow, and so eliminated much of the sagging and heaviness that form a serious drawback to walking on webbed snow- shoes over melting snow. Thongs coated with shellac also dried after becoming wet much more quickly than thongs micoated.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. HELENS CONSTABLE'S STRUGGLE.

Felled By His Own Truncheon. The story of a St. Helens policeman's terrific battle with two men was narrated at the local court on Monday. Timothy Kivelen, of Ex- change-street, St. Helens, was charged with a breach of the peace and assaulting Police-Con- stables Toft and Taylor. Thomas Stafford, labourer, also of Exchange-street, was charged with a breach • of the peace, assaulting the police, and with refusing to assist the police. "Things are getting very serious," said the Chief Constable. " I have never been in any place where they knock the police about as they do here." He added that there had been no fewer than forty assaults on the police, twelve of them bay- ing been during the past month. Kivelen took a running kick at Constable Toft and felled him. A terrible struggle then took place, and the constable called upon Stafford to assist him, but he refused, remarking "I won't help you if he kills you." Kivelen kicked the policeman in the stomach, and when the officer tried to blow his whistle Stafford snatched it from his hand. Kivelin next struck and kicked the officer in the face and on the body. The policeman tried to draw his staff, but Stafford took that from him and struck him several blows on the head with it, felling him to the ground. The constable was nearly powerless, and Kive- len again kicked him and ran off with the snaps hanging to one of his wrists. The plucky constable then tried to seize Staf- ford, whok kicked him on the face and body. He was only saved by Inspector Archers. Kivelen was sent to prison for twelve months with hard labour, and Stafford for nine months with hard labour.

THE HOUSE FLY.

Complains of Being Persecuted

But Promises

TO REHIN WITH US

"1 may be what you might call a dead one just now," said the House Fly, as he washed his face in his hands and wiped off his back with his hind legs. "At present you only find me alone and at intervals. I'm buzzing feebly in the warm corners of the steam-heated stable. I'm crawling languidly across the lathered face in the barber's shop. Back of the range in the table d'hote restaurant you detect me, awaiting my chance to fall into the entree and perish miserably. "But just wait. Just wait until the asphalt is hot enough to fry an egg, only nobody will crave fried eggs. Wait until the populace flee from the simmering city to the mountains which are even more simmeringer, if anything, or hie to the seashore where the Sanded strand is washed by the beating waves which, on going away, leave behind the quaint sea shell, the ancient watermelon rind, and the jellyfish that failed to jell and so died. Wait until you land in the screenless hotel bedroom and sit -flown to dinner in the porch dining-room. Then You'll find me —taking my Meals with you, spending the long afternoons with you, when you're trying to take a nap, leaving my characteristic signature on the salt cruet and crowding the sheet of sticky paper which forms so homelike a decoration of the breakfast-table, until nearsighted guests have been known to mistake it for currant cake. I'll be there with buzzers on then, believe me. "Mid I'll be increasing in unpopularity all the time, too. All mankind will be organised : against me. It seems I'm unpopular because I enter without knocking and come in without wiping my feet. The doctors will be preaching against me and the housemaids will be putting out poison, but I will go right on fulfilling my function in life and garnering the harvest. And so, no matter how unkindly you may speak of me and how hard you may try to exterminate• me, I'll be with you like your poor relations— even to the bitter end.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
"Nobody Worried Then."

"There was a time when mankina accepted me as a matter of course. That was before the day when the germ theory came to the front. But the scientists started out to prove that there was bacteria in everything from Bactrian camels to back drops, and so, naturally, they picked on me almost the very first one. Since 1896 I've been blamed for spreading everything from the free silver doctrine to the grippe." " Well," I inquired, as between us now, are you really as black as you're painted?" "You can search me," said the House Fly. "I'm blessed if I know. In the early days of medical development nobody gave me a thought. A few centuries back the family physician prac- tised his calling with the same tools that the plumber used. When a husky chap with his sleeves rolled up came to the maner hall, bear- ing in one hand a bucket of boiling tar and in the other a couple of saws and a cold chisel, they never knew, until they asked, whether he'd come to mend the ancestral water pipes or am- putate Me Lord's gouty leg. A little later it was the barber who combined cupping, leech- ing and cutting off legs on the bias with his regular calling. I suppose that explains why, when you go into a barber's shop and ask for a quick shave once over and no conversation, the barber tries to sell you an olive oil shampoo, a close haircut, a Turkish bath, a new way of trimming the moustache, and a bottle of hair restorer. It's the inherited instinct to bleed the customer that's coming to the surface again. "And, as I was saying, nobody worried about my pernicious activity then. Almost any old woman who lived alone and kept a black cat for company was regarded as an object of suspicion, but I went buzzing around as care-free and gal- lons as you please. If a patient got well, they said it was the physician's skill, and if he.died, they said it was an act of Providence, the same as they do now, and just let it go at that. No- body thought of putting the bee on me.

Doctors' Discoveries. "But bit by bit the doctors began' to advance. They discovered a fresh disease every day in the week except Sundays and holy days in Lent. They'd get up in the middle of the night to tag one, and give it a name that would scare any layman into coughing up ..t5 a visit. A citizen would come complaining of 'a rectangular kind of a pain in his interior, and the next thing you know the doctor would open the subject and look into it Thoroughly and come to the surface again with a new and highly expensive disease proudly clutched to his bosom. You may have noticed that they never by any chance ran on one that could be cured by a wet rag around the throat and a little mental effort. No, sir, it was always something that required excavating and a bunch of prescriptions which would en- able the struggling drug store man to clear off his second mortgage. It got to be the custom, if a man had a sort of untidy sensation down among his works, to take his life and his bank- roll in his hands and approach the operating- table on the run, ready to surrender one and sure to surrender the other. I think myself the X-ray was invented so the officiating surgeon could take a preliminary peep into the patient's clothes and see how much ready cash he was carrying. The doctors complain that they're the last to be paid, forgetting in the heat of the moment that a doctor's bill is so often the last one people ever get a.chance to pay.

" Well, anyhow, about the time all these things were coming to pass, somebody made the discovery that I was a bad little guy, training with a tough gang and ought to tie given hours to leave town in. My presence in the cut) cus- tard came to be regarded as not only uniesthetie, me not matching the rest of the goods, but actually unhygienic besides. And since then I haven't been able to give satisfaction anywhere. They'll tell you whiskers aren't sanitary, and that both towels operated on the Socialist plan won't do, and that everybody must have a separ- ate drinking cup—little things that never occurred to your ancestors as being important. And as for me, I'm regarded as the worst of the lot. But you'll take notice a little further along toward hot weather that I'm still able to sit up and take notice. I may have my enemies, but I also have my friends, and I expect to do a flour- ishing business at all the old stands this sum- mer. You'll find my signature on the hotel register, and my hyphen on the menu. I'll be one of the steady guests at every house party and no dinner table will be complete without the cheerful little buzz of my conversation and my dainty footprints in the butter."

BUILDING PROGRESS IN QUEBEC.

The building activities of Maisonneuve, in the Province of Quebec, Canada, indicate that this important city is far from suffering from commercial depression. One hundred thoirsand pounds sterling will be expended on new public buildings for Maisoi- neuve this season by the city and the Dominion Government. This will include the erection of a £60,000 post office on St. Catherine-street; a £16,000 fire station, and a civic bath house and gymnasium, for which £30,000 will be required. The fire station will be equipped throughout with motor-driven apparatus, and will be one of the most up-to-date in Canada. • Then three big factories costing about £200,000 will be erected, while £160,000 will be expended by the Vickers-Maxim Company, and another sum fully as large will be required for the con- struction of a big hippodrome.

-The work in sight -indicates that Maisonnenve will have one of the biggest years in its history.

Literary Gleanings.

COURTEOUS SCILLONIANS.

" Scilly" is the title of a book by Miss Jessie Mothersole, who gives some interesting facts about the people and the Scilly Islands. Refer- ence is made to the cheerful kindliness of dis- position of the inhabitants. This kindliness is shown in many little ways, even where there would be much excuse for contrary behaviour. A visitor and his wife were once trying to scale a stone fence—with no evil intent, but all the same it was trespassing. An islander who was working in the field, instead of stopping them offered to take off the top stones of the wall " to make it easier for the lady," and to replace them when they were safely over. The author speaks of their "delightful Celtic leasureliness." One kindly housewife only voiced the general feeling when she said, "Where's the need of hurry? What is not done to-day will be done to-morrow." An American girl once noticed this trait. " You haven't learnt to hustle here," said she to her boatman. "If the islands be- longed to us we'd soon make things spin." "Could you hustle the tide in and out?" was the quietly humorous retort. "Or could you hustle the fish into the nets and the lobsters into the pots?" Honesty is also a character- istic of the people. ".Once when visiting St. Agnes," says Miss Mothersole, " we were ad- vised to leave our luggage by a lonely road-side while we went round to look for rooms. We are all honest people on St. Agnes,' they told us, so it will be quite safe.' And so it was."

===== 0002601_19140801_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CANADIAN BOHUNK.

The bohunk is the name ''''''''''''' at work on railway construction in the Rocky Mountains. The following glimpse of their daily life in camp is taken from the "Canadian Magazine." At six in the morning the first bell rings. A camp bell is a six-foot drill bent into a triangle. On this the cookee clangs with another drill, or, where real music is appreciated, such as in an engineers' camp, with a wooden stick. That first bell never seems to fulfil any purpose n'a there is u t on a i m half-hour's at st p except as a reminder h hour's sleep longer. No- body rises. All the awakened sleepers do is to construction curse in their respective languages at the over- zealous efforts of the cookee. At six-thirty the last ringing alters things. The bohunk pushes aside his blanket, pulls on his boots, and is in the dining-room three minutes later. Some of them wash for breakfast. At the table they never talk. Most of them would not be able to make themselves understood by their neighbours. They just eat, earnestly, seriously, quickly, and plentifully. Invariably I was the last to leave the table, and Fletcher is not a personal friend of mine. While I would be helping myself to my first piece of pie the last bohunk would straggle out, leaving me to the hurrying glances of a dozen cookees impa- tient to commence preparations for the next meal. •

The day's work commences when the bohunk- presents himself at grade at seven, ready to walk or be carried to the place of operations. It may be an hour later before he wields a shovel—and when he does it requires some imagination to discover where. he earns his three dollars a day by his work. Some time before noon he quits work for the journey back to camp, where an hour is always allowed him, however long the. trip takes.

At night, unless there is a demand for special work (for which he receives extra pay), he is free to do what he likes. In that northern' country there is daylight in the summer until ten-thirty, and the bohunk is not the weary labourer of popular imagination. llis evening he fills with a visit to the end-of-steel village, or he loafs around the camp with another bohunk with whom he is able to converse.

In his fights he is more strenuous than in his work. An Italian and a Pole seldom see eye to eye when they happen to be sufficiently interested in each other to try it; and both exhibit little reluctance in filling up the chasm of conversa- tion with a knife or pistol. The Swede prefers his fists, the Italian a knife. The Pole and Russian a revolver, and the Hungarian uses anything from a rock to his teeth. Whichever is at hand is used solely with the idea of speedily ending .the engagement. The only sign of de- feat is a hors de combat condition. Without these fights the life of the bohunk Would be a wearisome existence.

At the end of his month—or perhaps. he re- mains two months—the bohunk is possessed of one idea. Railway construction, or, at least, construction lin that particular locality, is the worst job he has ever toiled at, he thinks. And the next clay finds him shouldering his baggage for the long tramp out.

KING'S PRIZE WINNER. Embraced by Ills Wife.

t A tie of 309 between an almost unknown ,Scot- tish marksman and a young ex-gold medallist whose father had won that honour before him provided an exciting finish for the King's prize at Bisley on Saturday. By scoring 10 points with his three tie shots, Sergt. J. L. Dewar, of the 4th Royal Scots, car- red off the Blue iliband of the world of rifle- shooting, defeating Private A. G. Fulton, of the Queen's Westminsters, by two points. Fulton won the King's Prize in 1912, and last week was the winner of the "Queen Mary's" and of the Silver Medal for the second stage of the King's. When the tie was shot off Dewar took the shot first and made a magpie. Fulton followed with a " bull," and Dewar got the same mark with his second. Fulton hesitated twice at his second shot, and altered his windage. 'A miss was sig- nalled. Dewar's third was an outer, but Fulton making only a magpie by his third lost by two points. Immediately the final shot had been registered the victor's young wife rushed forward and amid a great outburst of cheering the couple embraced each other in the dense crowd.

OYSTERS RETAILED BY NEW ZEALAND GOVERNMENT.

The Marine Department of the New Zealand Government has before now given evidence of an interesting spirit of enterprise in the ranks of its authorities and its latest adventure, that of retailing oysters to the public, is likely to be widely popular, with the public at least. Oysters can now be bought retail for a little over 2d. a dozen, the ordinary price being 4d. in Auckland. A State Depot has been estab- lished and orders are sent from all parts of New Zealand.

IMPROVED CROP OUTLOOK IN ONTARIO.

Crop conditions generally have improve(' materially during the past ten days in Ontario. The recent general rains did an immense amount of good. While the hay crop generally waS too far advanced to he helped very much, the spring grains were greatly benefited. Gener- ally speaking, the Outlook is fine, and given reasonably good conditions, a satisfactory crop is assured. In the southern counties of On- tario the prospects for fruit farmers are par- ticularly bright, and it is expected that the tomato harvest will be the most bountiful in many years. Contrary to an earlier report the peach crop along Lake Erie will be an abundant one.

IN STORM AND CALM.

Calm and still in glassy splendour Lay the ocean Not the tiniest little ripple, Not a motion. Yet by night a storm arises, Billows roar, Waves like mountains break in fury On the shore. In a tempest, or just rippling On the sand, The mighty ocean's in the hollow Of God's hand.

THE BIRDS THAT WAIT.

I am going to tell you of the poor little birds, which we miss so much through the winter..

In summer we love to hear the -little birds sing. It is lovely to lie under the trees and listen to our little friends. When the cold winter comes with Jack Frost and the snow, what have the little birds to do? Whenever there are signs of coldness they bid us farewell, for a time, and go to a country where there is no snow or cold.

When the birds come to the large seas which they have to cross, the very small ones get on the backs of the larger ones, and are thus car- ried across. To brighten the journey across the seas the small ones sing to the large ones. All the birds do not leave us. The dear little robins and the sparrows stay behind to keep us company through the dreary winter. These little birds have to find their food among the snow in the hard ground, and it is very difficult for them.

Last year I read in our Children's Page many good ways of feeding the birds. I tried one or two of these ways, and soon found that I had some regular visitors. I am going to do the same every winter, and I am sure some of the boys and girls who read the Children's Page will repay the little birds for remaining with them during the winter, and not seeking their own comfort abroad.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
A TRUE THRUSH STORY.

Mother and Father Thrush had chosen well their home-site, and nothing seemed likely to disturb the nursery cosiness of the fledglings— till I came. A wanderer by birth and occupa- tion those paths unknown to man call me irre- sistibly. Thus it was that I came across the ' willow-tree home of Peter, and I took him. He seemed not to mind one little bit, but to take to the change in his circumstances and surround- ings as if they were quite normal. Yet a long Journey in a cardboard box and a bed of cotton wool seemed a long way removed from his woodland home and warm mother's breast. He slept—that first night of our acquaintance—in the wool-lined starch box and awakened full early next morning, uttering insistent cries of impatience for his early breakfast. The tiny featherless creature stumbled boldly that morn- ing, and thereafter, across my pillow, ere the sun was fully risen, seeking the food of which he was in need. Peter grew bigger; feathers came instead of the soft down with which he was first covered; and be took to living in the garden, warily watching and listening— head on one side—to the earth-worms •on the close-shaved lawn, or hunting among the bushes and borders for the fat slugs which his soul desired.

A cage, with ever-open door, hung on the garden fence for his shelter and protection, but until night fell Peter never entered it. Peter loved human beings, and was on the friendliest terms with the comical-looking little woolly mongrel dog of the house. It was a very usual thing for Peter to take his noontide siesta inside Geoffrey's kennel, and in answer to anxious calls of careful friends he would come hopping briskly from its recesses to receive his daily dole of tit-bits. In winter-time Peter would stay in the kitchen, where he would hop about the floor and run great risks of being trodden on. In the winter evenings Peter and Geoffrey shared the kitchen heartbrug, and Peter always insisted on having his bath then, close to the big, glowing fire. Thus he lived with us for nearly five years, growing more and more inti- mate and friendly. _

Ile was never -frightened; casual guests and acquaintances were wonder-struck at the friendly familiarity of the little bird, and little children loved him. He would go impetuously to any child that came into his domains, and never once was Peter ill-treated. It was a cat that wrought Peter's destruction. By some mis- chance the door of his cage was closed when Peter, having stayed out till dusk, came hop- ping across the lawn to where his mistress was watering her plants, asking in his quaint hut expressive bird-language for the door to be opened that he might retire for the night. " One minute, Peter, till I have finished with this can, and emptied the water !" Alas, that fatal minute!

A rush, a scream, and the tiny life was ended! A big, ugly yellow cat was scrambling over the garden fence, and a tiny heap of brown and speckled feathers lay on the grass. There is another nest in the old willow tree this year, and other birds are ready to wing their way into the wide world, but our little friend Peter has left a memory too dear. to be wiped out by other bird friends, and his place on our old lawn will henceforth be vacant.—

NAVIGATION ON THE ST. LAWRENCE.

In connection with the inquiry into the Em- press .of Ireland disaster, the Marine Depart- ment at Ottawa has before it a number of recommendations and suggestions connected with the navigation of the St. Lawrence. One of these is that colliers and ships of that class shall follow a somewhat different track from that of the first-class steamships where the river is sufficiently wide to permit of this. An- other proposal is that inward and outward traffic be divided, inward westbound making Father Point light, while outward-bound ships steer to a point ten miles off the light, the tender with passengers ,and mails to wait for them there.
