A Glass Cleaner.—A preparation which will leave glass perfectly clean is sometimes very useful to the. photographer. Water, 5 ounces; methylated spirit, 1 ounce; liquor ammonia, ounce; tripoli powder, ounce. The water and spirit are shaken together, the ammonia is added, and then the tripoli powder. The mix- ture • may be kept in a bottle for use as re- quired. A little is rubbed well over the glass, allowed to dry, and then polished off with a clean cloth.—" Photography and Focus." SILVER-STREET.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
AMATEUR PHOTOGRAPHY.

The Precautions to be Taken When Mounting.

PERMANENCE OF PRINTS.

As photographers we are very fond of talking of the permanence of prints, as though we had no other consideration to bear in mind than the constitution of the photographic image. The effect of time with the train of deleterious in- fluences it brings is often lost sight of. We have, for example, the enemy mildew, which attacks not only photographs, but perhaps to a greater extent engravings, water-colours, and other art productions. We have seen old silver prints under cut mounts, the print in a good state of preservation and the mounts speckled all over with spots of mildew. It is probable that the early photographic workers took especial care to employ pure mounting boards, paste treated with some antiseptic preservative, and, when framing their work, to paper the glass into the frame so as to exclude not only dust and moisture, but the spores or other means of 'propagation of the, fungoid growths which we refer to as mildew. Nowadays the mounting boards commonly used are made of very poor stuff indeed, very largely of wood pulp or esparto grass, if we may judge from ' the rapid way in which they discolour when exposed to light. The board is surfaced with smooth paper, but we have no means of know- ing What putrescence there was in the adhesive employed. It has often been remarked that any mounting board will do for carbon or platinum prints, as they are permanent, but the mildew possibility has been overlooked , when this remark is made. The dry-mounting method with the waterproof shellac tissue, of course, insulates the print quite effectively, and would, we believe, be valuable in the case of water-colours. Another point not usually ! thought of is the cleaning of the glass of the ! frame. It is quite possible for the fungoid growths to be on the glass even after cleaning, unless precautions are taken to sterilise the glass in some way. Ordinary methylated spirit mixed with ammonia is a good cleaner, but a rub over with formalin would Tie an additional precaution and might save much trouble at a later date. It is, however, only the conscien- tious worker who will go to this trouble to save trouble in years to come.—" British Journal of Photography."

FOR NEXT SUMMER'S HOLIDAY.

The 290 annual meeting of the United King- dom!Pholographic Convention was concluded at Perth on July 11th, and it was decided to make Caen in Normandy the crtre of the meeting ! for '1915. The " A.P." says: It is a town of about the same size as Perth, hut its attractions for the pictorialist are of a different character. The streets are wide, there are shady boule- vards, many fine churches, some of them of beautiful Gothic architecture, as wel as the old timber houses and stone mansions with graceful dormer windows. With the stone from ! Caen many of our own 15th and 16th century churches are built. Photographers could hardly meet in a more picturesque place than this fine old town on the River Orne, and not only will it open up new ground to the camera, but it will afford satisfaction to the historical sense of standing at the source of that stream of Nor- man blood which has coursed through the VelllS of our own English history."

'CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

A Disappointing Season. The London season of 1914, which ends with Goodwood, has, for political reasons, been far from a brilliant one, and matrimonially also it has fallen short of expectations. Customs and manners, as well as fashions, change with every generation, and the London season is not now limited to the spring and summer. There is much more gaiety, and more entertaining in the autumn and winter than formerly. The rest- lessness, hurry, and swift travelling of these times naturally affect the " season " adversely, and combine to make it of shorter duration, and perhaps less brilliant. Greater things are expected of the season of 1915, when the Prince ef Wales fully comes of age, and the Princess 31ary will take her part in Court and other functions. The Prince is looking forward to his stay on the Continent, when he will probably make his first visit to Greece. The Prince has become quite keen on dancing, more especially the waltz, and all the members of the Greek Royal Family are noted for their love of danc- ing.

The American Tea House. The American idea of a tea-house in groands or park of a country house has been adopted by several of our hostesses this summer. Some- times the building is a classical temple, some- times a rural cottage, or a tiny house in keep- ing with the style of architecture of the "big house." But it always consists of a hall, a tea- room and a little kitchen, perfectly equipped with electric stove and every other appliance for the making of tea, which duty the hostess undertakes herself. A garden in most instances surrounds the little house. A " Wise Woman's " Prophecy.

Parisians are just now recalling the prophe- cies of a "wise woman," who, some fifty years ago, foretold the destruction of the beautiful city on the Seine, through the agency of the many subterranean springs underlying it. The crust over these appears to be caving in on all sides, and the authorities are quite unable to cope with the difficulty of making good the

===== 0002601_19140801_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMS. FOR MOTHERS.

Many young mothers, in their pride in the perfections of their first-born, quite forget—or rather perhaps are ignorant of a few of Nature's facts—that a baby is not a toy to be played with and tossed about like a doll. It cannot, therefore, be too strongly insisted on that, as a mother told her daughter, a baby is not a plaything, and give her hints in her own words. - - _

"It cannot be treated like one without disas- trous results following. "For the first six months of its life an infant can hardly be kept too quiet. " It should not be played with at all. "It shouldn't be talked to.

" I won't say it shouldn't be kissed, that would be expecting too much; but the less it is kissed the better.

"It can't sleep too much for the first half- year. "It should not be sung to. Don't imagine that you are 'developing its faculties ' by encourag- ing it to 'take notice,' as nurses call it—you are doing just the reverse; you are exciting its im- mature brain, a very mischievous thing to do.

_ f` All this may seem very hard lines to you, when you do so want to play with your new treasure; but parents have to do a lot of self- sacrificing, if they value their children's future. You make a bad start for baby in the race of life if you begin by exciting his brain, which is growing faster through the first two years he is alive than it will ever grow again, if he should live for a hundred years."

COMPLETE EXAMINER STORY.

THE HAUNTED HUNTING LODGE.

S. BARING-GOULD (Author of " Mehalah," " Curious Myths of the Middle Ages," etc.).

Not many travellers visit Darmstadt, and -of those who .do few remain over a day in perhaps the ugliest capital in Europe, and in the one where there is least of interest. Still fewer have made excursions in the neighbour- hood, and yet there are things to be seen out- side this hideous town, that deserve a visit. But we are not about to describe these, with the sole exception of one spot, the site of the Hunting Lodge of Grieshelm, six miles from Darmstadt, and reached nowadays by a steam tram. The place is now a camp and artillery range; but here and throughout the neighbourhood of Darmstadt are extensive forests. In the midst of one of these forests at Griesheim the Landgrave Ernest Ludwig ereoted a hunting lodge in 1716. It stood and was occasionally occupied by In succes- sor, Ludwig VIII., who died suddenly in the theatre as an actor on the stage pronounced the words, "May God have mercy on my soul!" This was in 1768. In 1770 the house was destroyed, even its foundations plucked up, by order of his successor, Ludwig IX., and the Landgrave required the head coun- cillor of the Board of Forestry to draw an account of the singular circumstances which induced him to order its demolition. It is from this protocol that the following nar- rative is taken.

The site of the lodge may still be seen. In the midst of the forest is a mound, octagonal, level at the top and the platform covered with gravel. In the midst stands a stone eight-sided, and radiating from these sides are as many alleys cut through the forest. On this mound, once considerably larger than at present, stood the hunting-lodge, once the scene of merry parties, when hounds bayed, horns were sounded, and the Landgrave and his noble friends in gallant costume of green and gold started for the chase, or to which they returned for a cold collation, when much wine flowed, and the ball resounded -with songs and toasts, and merry voices relating hunting experiences and adventures. Beneath the lodge, extend- ing its full length and breadth was a roomy cellar. Above the foundations the whole structure was of oak, painted green. On the north side was the entrance leading into a hall or passage from which led a flight of stairs to the upper storey. On the ground floor was a large saloon on one side, and on ;the other another apartment. These wers well furnished with the walls hung with antlers, hunting pictures, mirrors and aide lights. There were as well porcelain stoves. The windows were curtained, and provided with stout shutters outside, that were fast- ened from within. Upstairs was beside other chambers, a small kitchen. And above the roof rose tall brick chimneys. Apart from the house on the mound were stables and kennels for the hounds and charn- bers for the foresters, but these were occu- pied only on the occasion of a hunt in the Grieswald Forest. As there was no stream

Under Ernest Ludwig, the Master and Sup- erintendent of the Forests was a Baron von Mingerod. The Landgrave Ernest Ludwig was the builder of the huge and ugly palace at Darmstadt, far too large for such a small land. The Baron von Mingerod served from 1712 to 1749, partly under this Landgrave, who died in 1739, and partly under his suc- mssor, and died in 1750 without issue. He was noted as a peculiarly harsli and severe Upper Forester, punishing with the utmost strictness and without compassion not only the poachers in the preserves, but also • the poor peasants who were caught collecting the fallen wood for fuel. He was greatly hated, but perhaps he only did what he was bound to do—and when he died, the Landgrave Lud- wig composed a poem on him expressive of the esteem in which he held the Baron. Not long after his death, strange stories began to circulate relative to apparitions in the neighbourhood, and in the very hunting lodge itself. It was said that suddenly on the stillest night a blast of wind would sweep round the lodge, rend the trees, and tear the tiles from the roof. These whirlwinds rose at midnight, raged for a few minutes, and then all was still again. Passers-by saw at night the windows brilliantly illuminated, al- though it was known that no one was within, and that the shutters were fast. Many people declared that they had seen the dark form of the dead Baron on his horse riding through the wood. A fo'rester passing along one alley when the moon was full, saw stand- ing in the way a monstrous nu-hound. Full of athazement at seeing such a beast un- known at Darmstadt, he halted. His own dog crouched whining and terror stricken be- tween his feet. Gathering up his courage he advaneed, when thm boar-hound became in- distinct and nebulous, and he walked through it These stories circulated, and few people would venture at night. near the hunting lodge.

They were engaged on it all day till evening. Then they fastened the shutters, ex- tinguished the lights, locked the doors and retired. They had not gone a hundred and fifty paces from it before they, and an elderly man who had been sent with them, turned to 100. back. To their unspeakable amazement, they saw that the shutters had been thrown open and that the windows were full of bril- liant light. There was no moon, there was no possible natural explanation of the pheno- menon, and they hurried on their way in the greatest alarm. Even when they were at a considerable dis- tance, along one of the straight alleys, and looked back, they could still distinguish the illumination. If by inadvertence a light bad not been extinguished in one room, that would not explain the rays streaming from every window, and • those windows which had been closed by shutters. The Revival of Crystal and Steel Buckles. Crystal is just now in high favour, and as a delicate and refined ornament, has much to I recommend it. Old-fashioned crystal beads and pendants are being brought out of their Ming places, and many new designs are shown in the fashionable jewellers' shops. Bags of the ruffled kind have crystal lozenges hanging from the clasps, and crystal tubes cover the cords. Some- times a beautiful diamond is dropped, as it were, in a disc of crystal, and classical figures are beautifully engraved on crystal for pend- ants and medallions. Another old friend is the steel buckle that has come back again, and is used with excellent effect on white suede shoes. In Louis XVI. designs these ornaments are par- ticularly beautiful for evening shoes of satin or suede, and are used as much for morning as for afternoon and evening wear.

The Craze for Statuettes. Society's latest craze takes the form of col- lecting statuettes in wax, bronze or plaster, that come from Paris or Vienna, and are often very odd and quaint. A table for these is reserved in the drawing-room, and it is surprising how interesting a collection may become. It is not an expensive hobby, which is something in its favour. Rather gruesome are the latest mascots called Ershabti. They are copies of ancient Egyptian figures that were placed on tombs. Their duties were to act as the servants of the dead in the other world. Some are too long to be worn as ornaments, and find a use as paper 1 weights or ornaments for dressing tables.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
Mrs. Marshall's Orchestra.

••••-- - Mrs. Julian Marshall, originator and conduc- tor of the South Hampstead Orchestra, is to be commended for her indefatigable exertions in connection with the gradual perfection of this amateur band of musicians, three-fourths of whoe are women. It is only one more notable instance of women's work, and what women can accomplish in music as in other arts. This orchestra gave an admirable rendering of Brahm's Symphony in E minor at their annual concert, which took place in the Queen's Hall, an indisputable proof of their proficiency. The Late Summer Hat. _ _

_ As the London season comes to an end and outfits for travel abroad, the seaside, or country wear, absorb attention, a change always comes about in millinery. The newest hats are still quite small, and evidently designed for short people, for crowns are high, and the trimming still higher, although very moderate as to quantity, and con- sisting often of a narrow velvet band and a tuft of ostrich feathers perched on the crown. From these a very high mount sfiings, sometimes straight, sometimes curiously curved. These hats are decidedly smart, and but for the fan- tastic and excessively high mounts, might be pronounced sensible and practical. Some crowns curve outwards towards the top.

Petticoats, Gloves, and Tulle Scarves. Petticoats have come back with the slightly wider dresses, and the newest idea is to allow them to show below the hem of the skirt, or rather the lace with which they are trimmed shows below the dress. The effect is not un- pleasing, although scarcely practical for walk- ing costumes, and the average woman to-day esteems utility. The tulle scarves that are so much used for evening wear offer a mode of ob- taining variety with little expense. The shades of the tulle are lovely, and three or four scarves in different colours would not be an extrava- gance if added to a young girl's wardrobe dur- ing the sales, for when donned with the same white dress they afford change and relief. Café au lait is a favourite tint for gloves, both for day and evening wear, and it can be procured in the fabric glove, that every season ap- proaches nearer perfection, atifj is a blessing to

COOKERY CORNER.

A Recipe for Stuffed Cabbage.

Select a fine head of cabbage; boil till tender but quite whole, and drain. Make a stuffing of two tablespoonfuls of boiled rice, Vb. of sausage, a teaspoonful of chopped onion, and a table- spoonful of parsley; mix well. Open the cab- bage to the centre, put in a teaspoonful of the mixture, fold over the next layer of leaves, put in more stuffing, and continue until each layer is 'stuffed. Press firmly together, tie up, put into a baking tin, with a good lump of butter, bake in a hot oven twenty minutes, basting it frequently. Stand the cabbage in a deep dish, pour melted butter over, and serve.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
Noisettes of Mutton or Beef.

Cut up the lean part of a well-hung neck of mutton or some pieces of fillet of beef. Cut the meat in slices about fin. in thickness, and beat them with a heavy, wet knife. Trim neatly into squares or rounds, season with coralline pepper, a little finely minced parsley, shallot and salt, and a few minced mushrooms if ob- tainable; put them in a well-buttered frying- pan, and toss them quickly for three or four minutes, first on one side then on the other. Fry a long, narrow crouton of bread, put on this some slices of tomato, which have been cooked in a greased tin in the oven, season them to taste, arrange the noisettes between the sliCes of tomato, and pour some tomato sauce round.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
A Popular Craft.

Woven rafia work is much used just now for heads of hatpins, belts, hatbands and chair covers. A simple frame that can be made at home is all that is required for the weaving. Very durable mats for verandahs or gardens are made from roped of plaited rafia. Smyrna rug canvas, Java canvas and Russian crush are used for effective embroideries and cross stitch in this new and interesting handwork. Some of the mats made from dyed and woven rafia look like pony skin, others resemble smooth grey leather. The popularity of this work is doubtless ac- counted for by the scope it affords for carrying out individual ingenuity and originality.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
lIINTS ON HEALTH.

Dangers of Tetanus.

In a lecture at the Royal Institute of Public Health on "Thirty Years in the History of Tetanus," M.L. Vaillard said the tetanus bacil- lus was found in the spore condition in the soil, in particles of dust flying in the air, and in mud from surface waters. Its diffusion around us was enormous. The ground was the most favourable place; it was to be found widely spread there. Its presence was common, almost constant, in the street sweepings, garden soil, and fields which had been inanured. It was rarely found deep down, but was abundant on the surface.

The soil and all the objects contaminated by it could therefore be a source of infection of the virus. It was by means of the soil that the spores of the tetanus bacillus were carried into habitations; hence their presence in common amongst floor dust and dust from walls. Tetanus spores might be found on the surface of vege- tables, where they were carried by the dust and debris of the soil. Tetanus could easily be trans- mitted to animals by inoculating them subcu- taneously with hay dust.

If a wound were contaminated with tetanus spores, but at the same time no bacteria favour- able to their germination gained access, no tetanus would result; this was the reason why tetanus was rare, in spite of its enormous dis- persion. The result was that the most rigorous antiseptic treatment of all wounds, even the slightest ones, would reduce the chances of in- fection to a minimum.

Of her Wieland said, " If I were King of Fate for a moment, I would at once pro- claim her Queen of all Europe." The Landgrave and his wife laughed at the stories, as idle tales. Now he had a regi- ment of picked men, his white dragoons, who stood high in his favour. In this regiment was a Captain Fuchs, who had formerly been in the Prussian Service, had been engaged in the war in Silesia, and had been decorated by Frederick the Great for his distinguished bravery. Fuchs had heard the stories relative to the haunting of the lodge, and he de- dared that he would put an end to them, if the Landgrave would let him have his way. The prince heard of this, and hoping to put an end to the superstitious alarm among the people, he ordered Captain Fuchs into his presence and asked him what he would do. Fuchs replied that he held no belief in ghosts and that he would soon find out if trickery were at the bottom of it all. Let him have a score of his best dragoons, pickea men in tne regiment, to spend the night in the lodge, let them have rood and wine and plenty of light, and he would defy the devil. " Twenty is rather a large order," pro- tested the Landgrave. " Can't possibly do it with less," said Fuchs, and Ludwig consented, only insisting that the matter should not get wind. Fuchs accordingly did not allow his pur- pose to transpire till the day on which the attempt was to be made to rout the ghosts out of the hunting lode. Then he chose twenty of the most reliable men, told them his purpose, and said, " Now, any one of you White Dragoons who is inclined to show the white 'feather, let him withdraw at once; there are plenty of other good fellows who will be willing to take his place." Not a man begged off, and the little com- pany marched with their guns and sabres to the lodge in the wood, whilst it was still day- light. Every command I give is to be promptly and exactly performed, and," he laid a couple of pistols on th? table, " the man who deserts his post, or in any way dis- obeys orders, I will shoot Sim. dead."

WIDNES WORKERS' NARROW ESCAPE.

The flooded state of the Mersey during the stormy weather of Sunday morning caused ex- tensive damage at the West Bank Dock, Widnes, where anew entrance, to facilitate the passage of shipping, is being constructed. Shortly after midnight a high tide and wind swept away a large dam, and the whole of the working's were flooded to a considerable depth. The rush of water was so strong that large steel piles were bent over 2ft. from the perpendicu- lar. Until the dam can be repaired the docks are dependent upon the rise and fall of the tide, and this will greatly interfere with shipping. Several workers just escaped a moment before the dam was flooded. Considerable damage has been done.

By Captain Fuchs's orders every candle in the house was lighted. There were triple branches and sconces belore mirrors in the saloon. Lights were also placed on the tables, and every passage and room was illumined; but the apartments on the upper floor were closed, the doors being locked and the keys taken away by Fuchs. The Captain remained seated at a table in the saloon, with four loaded pistols before him; but occasionally he walked through the other rooms or went outside. Midnight approached, and the re- lieved outpost soldiers reported that riothing had occurred to arouse suspicion. The first to be alarmed was the sentinel planted at the furthest point to the squth-west. He heard a dull rustling in the distance as though the wind were stirring the fallen leaves. It ap- proached, and suddenly a furious whirlwind spun about the house bending the pines and rending branches. And to increase the alarm of the sentinels they saw streaks of fire pass through the forest, approach, and -whirl about their heads. Panic struck, they all de- serted their posts and scampered into the lodge. The appearance of a legion of devils would have been more welcome to the Captain, than that of his eight sentinels deserting their posts. He also had heard" the roar of the wind, and now he outreared it in curses and rebukes to the cowardly and indocile dra- goons. He raised a pair of his pistols and swore to shomt them unless they at once re- turned to their posts. The men tremblingly obeyed. The storm still raged, and blue flames danced on the wind, ran up the walls' of the house, and flick&ed over the chimneys.

Then at Midnight all at once all was still again, and remained -,;o for a while. The sentinels were changed, and those who re- turned drank pretty heavily to recover from the fright to which they had been subjected. Captain Fuchs was in a bad humour be- cause he had to do tith the elements, and not with spectres. Suddenly in the stillness sounded a shot. then another, and again another. The Cap- tain sprang from his seat, seized a pair of pistols, and rushed out of the house, followed by his men. Nothing could be heard and no- thing seen, however the eyes were straintd looking into the darkness. They all returned to the house, awaiting the moment of change of sentinels to hear the report of the men. At that morant a dragoon, named Zeitner, a native of uric- sheim entered to infOrm Captain Fuchs that one of the sentinels planted nearest to the lodge had deserted his post. The Captain strode to the door to convince himseii if this were a fact, when something occurrod that arrested his steps. With a report like the bursting of a bomb, the -wind struck the house that rocked to its foundations, every

window and door was dashed open, and eters light extinguished. Then, as suddenly, the wind lulled.

A. light was struck and the candles were rekindled, when to the amazement of all, not a door nor a window was found to be open, and everything was in its place. The Captain racked his brain to explain this puzzling event, but his conjectures as to a cause were cut short by a glance at the clock which showed him that the time for a change of sentinels was over-passed by twelve minutes. He mustered the dragoons but only six were present, and as he supposed that the other six were in the second apaitment on the ground floor or in the passage, he sent a veteran non- commissioned officer named Hermann to sum. mon. them into the saloon. Hermann returned in a minute or two tc say *that they were not in the house, nor had any of them answered his summons when he called them by name outside the door. There- upon, highly incensed, the Captain, attended by four men, left the saloon, taking with them a lantern and went forth at the prin- cipal door of the house. As thy searchea all round the hunting lodge they called the men. Not only were 'the six who had been in the howse gone, but every sentinel had disap- peared. Fuchs had accordingly to return to the house and content himself with watching that.

Some hours passel, and the hand of the cloc.i: pointed to thr e, when a whispering and hissing and moaning accompanied by a shrill voice raised in mocking ontchter heard pasting round the house. The Captain went forth to the doorstep and shouted:— If all the devils of hell Came to take up their quarters hare, I will not bue.ge from the place!" Hardly was the last word out of his mouth when a blow as from a whirlwind smote him on the back, issuing from within the house, and precipitated him to the bottom of the flight of steps that led to the front door. At the same moment every light in the lodge was again extinguished. Again they were plunged in Egyptian darkness, and then ab. raptly the wind ceased and all was still. The Captain now himself seems to have lost his nerve, for on consultation with his men, now reduced to four, two more dragoons hav- ing disappeared, it was unanimously agreed to quit the haunted lodge. The Captain with four men, what could he do ? Accordingly all the lights were extinguished, the doors locked, and the little troop, at about a quar- ter past three in the morning, took its way towards Kranichstein where were their quar- ters, and where was the , Landgrave at the time.

But what had become of the sixteen dra- goons of the famous Darmstadt regiment of picked men ? Had they fallen victims to de- mons ? By no means, they had all run away, frightened out of their wits, and had not stayed their feet till they reached their bar- racks, where they told a, strange tale. The eight who had been on the outpost had seen mysterious blue flames dancing over the soil and flitting in the air. They had seen a huge fiery mass move out of the wood and approach the lodge. whereupon thcz had discharged their carbines at It, and "had then taken to their heels. Such is the story. Immediately after this event, the Landgrave had the hunting lodge pulled down. Whether it were because of the haunting or because his picked dragoons had displayed the white feather there, is not stated. It does not seem—at the least it is not recorded—that the runaway dragoons were punished for their conduct.

Strange Incident in Warrington.

4... .y,.. .

Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest at Warring- ton on Saturday on the body of John Miller (27), carter, lodging at 18, Lion-street, who was found hanging in an outbuilding in the yard of Messrs. Buckley and Co., where lie was employed. . Mrs. Long said deceased, who had lived with her for about eight months, was very quiet and had very little to say about himself or his family. He left home on Thursday morning at 5.30, and at night she heard he had hanged him- self.

The Coroner: Had he not been strange in his manner?—He was always very quiet. I did not know he had any trouble.

And he did no speak much ?-11.e very seldom spoke and sometimes he would not answer when addressed.

That is very strange, especially with regard to your sex. (Laughter.) Witness added that the did not know who de- ceased's parents were. Deceased told her that his father was at Altrincham. Detective-Sergeant Holland: The Altrincham police cannot find any 1 person of that name re- lated to Miller.

William Sudlow, yard foreman for Messrs. Buckley and Co., and residing at 96, Church- street, spoke to finding deceased, who had a rope round his neck and his cap under his chin. He had noticed that Miller was very quiet. He was one of the best workmen at the place. John Muskett, clerk for Messrs. Buckley and Co., said that along with the last witness he cut deceased down.

The Coroner: Do you know of any trouble he had?—No, sir.

You knew of no reason why he should do this? —No. He was very quiet and reserved, and never said much to anyone. P.C. Plumb said he found £9 15s. 91d. in the pocket of deceased.

The Coroner (th the jury): It was not through money.

P.C. Plumb: There was no correspondence re- lating to anyone. The Coroner: I don't suggest embezzlement, but was the money his own or the company's? Mr. Muskett : It was his own.

It was not his business to collect money?—No, sir, it was his own.

A Juror said he been with the deceased the previous Saturday night, and he said he was "down." He (witness) said: You cannot be down when you have money in your pocket." He was very quiet and appeared upset, but would not say what over. The Foreman: Did you know he had money then?—Certainly. The Coroner: Did he look a bit queer 2—With- out a doubt, but I could not make it out. Had he been a heavy drinker?—No, decidedly not.

Did he take it to excess—Well, anyone will have a glass of beer if they want it. The Coroner: I will give way to your superior judgment (Laughter.) Mrs. Long: I never saw him the worse for drink.

A verdict of "Suicide whilst of unsound mind" was returned.

A GRAVEYARD ON FIRE.

Flames and dense smoke were seen issuing from the graveyard surrounding the Gaelic Church of St. Columba at Paisley on Saturday. The churchyard, about half an acre in area, had been strewn with cut hay, which was lit by a spark from a railway engine. Firemen drenched the graveyard with water.

Crosfield), Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall, and Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. Continuing, Mr. Patrick said they offered .a hearty welcome to the Mayoress. (Applause.) It was the first time in the history of that Asso- ciation that they had been honoured by the presence of the Mayoress of the town at a meet- ing of that descrivtion. He could assure her that she was in safe company—(laughter)—for that she had no more loyal subjects in the town than those who belonged to that Association. ' (Hear, hear.) If it were needed they would all stand between ,her and death. He spoke that from a universal standpoint. Working men'of Warrington respected her, and recognised that she and her husband had great responsibilities on them, and he could assure the Mayor and Mayoress that they were ready and willing to back them a up. He had great pleasure in congratulating the winners of the "FinlaFson" Cup, and he must also console with the losers, who were out for the first time. He hoped they would be more successful next year. He had to thank the various secretaries and captains of the teams, who assisted in conducting the business of the Association in an amicable way.

MR. A. 11. CROSFIELD AND BOWLING. His Interest in the 'Works Association.

Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the Mayoress, and at the outset com- mented on the unsettled state of the weather, and said that the moment the Mayor and Mayoress appeared on the scene the weather im- proved. (Hear, hear.) They all knew that they had never had a Mayor and Mayoress like them nor in any other town 111 old Lancashire. (Cheers.) They were übiquitous and could never wear out their welcome wherever they went. On behalf of those present he gave them a hearty welcome that afternoon. (Applause.) He could assure the Mayor and Mayoress that all of them in Warrington, without distinction of party, creed or anything of that kind, re- spected and valued the indefatigable work they were putting in for the good of the town. (Ap- plause.) -

A PLEASANT TEA.

The teams were afterwards entertained to tea by the Association, and Mr. J. Willett, in pro- posing the toast of the winning team, congratu- lated all concerned in the competition on the good feeling which had been shown throughout. Mr. T. Boyd. in seconding, heartily congratu- lated the winners on their success. He thought his team were capable of winning, but after that clay they were every one willing to admit that they. had been beaten by a better team. He promised that a team would enter the competi- tion another year. Whoever had the honour of leading them would have, as he had had this year, a team of triers. Votes of thanks were proposed to the officials by Mr. J. Mehers, which were responded to by Mr. J. Willett, Mr. W. Patrick and Mr. A. E. Burdett; and to Mr. and Mrs. Rushworth for their excellent catering, to which Mr. Rush- worth suitably responded.

1.0.0. F., 11. U.

Annual Bowling Handicap.

The annual bowling handicap for the " Sir Gil- bert Greenall " Challenge Cup and other valu- able prizes in connection with the Warrington aistrict. of the above order will take place this afternoon on the Bull's Head Hotel Bowling Green, Church-street.

The officials are desirous of making a prompt start at two o'clock, and any member not an- swering to his name within 30 minutes of the same being called in the first round will be disqualified. No time will be allowed after the first round has been completed. The Challenge Cup will be presented to the winner at the close of the handicap by the Grand Master of the Warrington District, Bro. Harry Lawrence, but the other prizes will be presented at a smoking concert to be held at a later date, full particulars of which will be sent to the different lodges. The total number of entries is 81. Bros. William Butterworth and James A. Melbourne have been appointed to act as referees and P.G. William E. Clare is again the secre- tary of the handicap.

GREAT SANKEY.

WEDDING OF MISS DUNIBILL.

A Pretty Ceremony at St. Mao's

Church.

At St. Mary's Church on Wednesday afternoon the marriage was solemnised of Miss Ruth Dum- bill, daughter of Mr. H. Dumbill, Warrington- road, Penketh, and Mr. T. 11. Humphreys, of Fowl Farm, Cuerdley. The bride, who was given away by her uncle, Mr. E. Dumbill, was prettily attired in a white silk dress, the hem outlined with seed pearls; a draped bodice trimmed with lace and orange blossom, and a train trimmed with sprays of orange blossom and seed pearls. Her veil was of Brussels net mnbroidered, and she wore a wreath of orange blossom, and carried a shower bouquet of white roses, carnations and smilax. The bridesmaid, Miss E. Humphreys (sister of the bridegroom) was dressed in pale blue silk, with hat to match. Mr. John Humphreys, brother of the bridegroom, acted as best man. The service was fully choral, Miss Standish presiding at the organ, and the officiating clergyman being the Rev. J. H. Jones, of the Parish Church.

_ _ The reception afterwards was held at Woodend Farm, Cuerdley, the home of the bride and bridegroom, where about seventy guests were entertained.

Presents were received from the following : Father and mother of the bride, a drawing - room suite; the mother of the bridegroom, a mahogany bed and cushions; Mr. J. Humphreys and Miss Webster, white quilt; Mrs. Knowles, rug; Miss Humphreys and Mr. Owen, toilet set; Mrs. J. Humphreys and Miss Oscar, eider- down quilt; Mr. and Mrs. Humphreys, dinner service; Misses M. and E. Humphreys, wicker chair; Mr. A. Humphreys, jardiniere; Mr. and Mrs. Knowles,' jardiniere; , Edie and Walter, jardiniere; Mr. J. Humphreys and Miss War- burton, fur rug; Uncle Dick and Aunt Francis, blankets; Mr. and Mrs. J. Davies, blankets; Aunt Annie and Uncle Peter, brass and ebony flower stand; Aunt Alice and Uncle John, ebony flower stand; Mr. and Mrs. W. Pemberton, hand-embroidered tablecloth; Mr. and W. Min- ton, table and tray cloth; Mr. and Mrs. E. Dum- bill, occasional table; Mr. F. Shaw, case of cutlery; Mr. W. Critchley, glass dishes; Uncle Jim and Polly, oak coal scuttle; Mr. 11. Byrom, case of silver fish carvers; Mr. and Mrs. J. Knowles, mirror and brushes; Mr. and Mrs. T. Dumbill, tea service; Mr. and Mrs. Emerson, tea service; Mr. and Mrs. W. Vorcott, silver tea spoons and sugar tongs; Mr. and Mrs. F. Dum- bill, knives and forks; Misses E. and P. Pusill, teapot and hot water jug; Mr. and Mrs. Dwerry- house, silver cruet; Martha and Ted, trinket set; Ronald and Ada, Doulton-ware vases; Mr. A. Harrison, silver and glass vases; Mrs. and Miss Gibbons, Worcesterware fruit dish and jug; Mr. H. Gibbons, teapot; Mrs. Gibbons and Mary, doll3rtub; Adam and Kitty, silver salt cellars. Two unknown friends, satin eiderdown and honeycomb quilts. The bride's trawalling dress was a tweed cos- tume and blue hat with plumes. The honey- moon is being spent at the Isle of Man.

COLONIAL WIT AND

HUMOUR.

It is sometimes said that all French jokes touch one subject, awl that the standard English jests are the "mother-in-law joke," and the lodging-house meal joke." About Australian humour, there is often refreshing variety and uncompromising bluntness; above all it never verges upon the border line of bad taste. The Colonial mirth-maker goes straight to the point, nor " beats about the bush;" on the other hand, his funny story is often apropos of the "Bush." Especially is this true of the healthy school of Australian poetn, which flourishes in the Island Contin- ent. One of the best known of these is Mr. A. B. Paterson, and, though a good deal of his verse deals with 'open air _ife " Down Under," you can find a number of really funny touches in his "Man from Snowy River," and his "Rio Grande's Last Race. This is his conclusion of a story of a whist novice who, in excusing his bad p:ay,, urged that he feared there might be a "fourteenth trump " out against him! - -

"They buried him at dawn of day Bcside a ruined stump; And there he sleeps the hours away, Aad waits for Gabriel to playl.- 'Me last—the fourteenth—rump." Or again, how funnily he tells the tale of the thirty and hard-up " sundowner," who comes to Walgett, by the riverside in the plain where "there isn't a stone within fifty mile." At Walgett the great joke is to per- suade the guileless stranger to wager that he can throw- a stone across the river; the na- tives lead this stranger on as usual. "The yokels laughed at his hopes o'er- thrown, As he stood awhile like a man in a dream; Then out of his pocket he fetched a stone, And pelted it over the silent stream— He had been there before—he had wandered

down On a previous visit to Walgett town." POLITICS AND SOCIETY. The " Sydney Bulletin," a weekly paper with its motto "Australia for the Australi- ans and bother anyone else," is as familiar and as time-honoured as our English " Punch." Its treatment of political matters, its comments on Society doings and its " An- swers to Correspondents," are marked by a perfectly marvellous outspokenness. It is im- possible to imagine any British journal thus describing a prosy orator at Westminster : "Mr. Bruce Smith had been speaking on the address for about four hours and forty minutes, and a very, very weary House yawned like a railway tunnel with a hurri- cane blowing through it." Or again, there is no lack of plain speaking in " Joseph Ward, Maoriland's Premier, -e- -turned from the Cold Country (England) showing signs of infection with the snobbery bacillus."

Society is treated with the same untram- melled Plainness. A local bride (who weds " a nice young man ") is referred to as " the eldest ewe-lamb of Mr. Beate-Hall," and an- other local magnate's daughters are "the Hordern batch;" while a loving couple is labelled "Mr. Tittell Brune and his much- worshipped missus." A few comments on Mr. A. J. Balfour may amuse. English readers accustomed to more veiled methods:—He " looked like a dissent- ing parson or a Quaker, with an almost basin-like thatch drawn back from his high bleak forehead. He yapped tiresomely and talked platitudes with any amount of vigour which couldn't be mistaken for magnetic fire." Not the most outspoken Radical jour- nal here would put the matter in so strong a fashion. There is a novelty about this, too:—" Mr. John S. Dane and his lately-acquired wife farewelled their respective progenitors and pals last weed, and went off world-wandering on the " Manuka." They made the sixth couple of billers and cooers aboard that blessed vessel."

Certain Tea Concerts are pointed out as possessing this advantage for ladies, viz., that they can "nut in a solid hour garnering the currants of -scandal front the bun of fact."

SIR BOWEN BOWEN-JOIE9" ADVICE,,

The annual prize distribution in connection with the Warrington Graniniar Scnool was held in the Parr Hall on Thursday afternoon. There was the usual large attendance of parents and friends of he children.

Sir Gilbert Greenall presided over a represen- tative platform. He paid a touching tribute to the late Alderman Forshaw, and said that few were more interested in their old school. •

real efficiency of the school largely depends. Before I conclude my reocrt, there are two other points I must refer to. First, I would sincerely acknowledge the confidence which the governing body so kindly continue to mani- fest in myself. This I most. highly appreciate. I cannot pass from this point without allud- ing to the loss the school bas so recently suf- fered in the decease of one of her governors, the late Alderman William J. Forshaw, who always displayed the keenest interest in our welfare.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0266.txt | included=true | included =====
HEAD MASTER'S REPORT. Another Successful Year.

In his report, which he addressed to t! chairman and governors, the Head master said At the close of the school year it is incum- bent upon me as head master to present to you a report which shall deal specifically with the general condition and progress of the school. It is now my pleasure to do so. •

The year now ended has been marked by no special feature, but the efficiency so character- istic of our school has been fully maintained. I ana anable to name any university distinc- tion gained in the year; indeed it woe' • .)em that comparatively few boys desire receive the necessary preparation for the university. By many the gaining of special scholarships is regarded as the test of the true worth of a school. But there is, if rightly understood, a higher test—that which has been termed "the mediocrity of the golden kind." We do not look to produce prodigies, but to do the greatest good to the greatest number, to make of aver- age boys, boys honest and healthy in mind, and to send forth useful and intelligent youths, ready to do good work in such spheres as open out Itafore them. Boys that I have known during the seven years I have served the school have referred to their own schooldays, when they questioned the value and the reason of the instruction imparted to them. I have no doubt it is the same now. I would say to such that "he who would learn must trust." We who are older can see the reason and know that beside training our boys to be careful and discriminating collectors of scholarship, we also look for the growth of other qualities— honour, duty, manliness, simplicity, &ad truth- fulness.

Sound Instruction Given. Dealing more intimately with the subjects which find place in our curriculum, which has been so devised as to cover.the recognised range, and which meets the requirements of boys who desire a generous secondary education, I ven- ture to express the hope that the report of the Oxford examiner will convince you that sound instruction has been given. Our classical tradi- tion has been fully preserved. French and German have had ample time allotted to them, while mathematics and science, as befits our environment, have had their importance duly impressed on the minds of the boys. I have myself been somewhat disappointed with the advance made in mathematics in the highest forms, but it may be that I look for a high standard. The English subjects have shown considerable improvement, although in the lower forms a great deal of time has perforce been devoted to the teaching of the simple elements of English grammar.

It is to be regretted that boys of the age of twelve and even thirteen years who come from the elementary schools to us are unable to ex- press themselves clearly in their mother tongue and to distinguish the parts of speech. This very materially hampers our" progress. Geog- raphy has been taught on modern scientific lines, due regard being paid to both the com- mercial and economic sides. The boys of the middle and lower forms have received manual instruction, and shorthand has been taught to those boys ,who expressed the wish to learn that subject.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0267.txt | included=true | included =====
Civil Service Successes.

During the year we have had another suc- cess in the Civil Service examinations. J. C. F. Fern, who was our head boy, gained a second division clerkship; C. W. Healey gained his place in the first division of the London Univer- sity matriculation—the list was only issued on Monday last. E. C. Fox, who on entering the school in 1910 was placed in the preparatory class, was elected to an open entrance scholar- ship at Stonyhurst College, heading the list. Thd Oxford local examinations were taken by the fifth form, and by those members of the sixth who had not previously taken the senior examination. All the candidates gained the certificate, and all the seniors secured first-class honours. Of these C. W. Healey was excused responsions, and distinguished in religious knowledge. Two junior candidates, G. Murray and D. E. Wright, were placed in the first class, the former gaining distinctions in French and Greek.

The school, during the past fortnight, has once again been examined and inspected by the Oxford delegacy, the examiner, who has been resident with us, being T. Bernard Baker, Esq., ALA., Oxon. His report you have heard, and I trust you consider the same to be emin- ently satisfactory. He, I may say, told rile personally that having had 15 years' experi- ence in examining grammar school S; he con- sidered our school to be well above the average.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0268.txt | included=true | included =====
Old Boys' Honours.

Coming to the successes of the Old Bote- lerians, there is a very considerable list, which I hope I have complete. J. Bamforth, R. Ken- non, and H. Nield have proceeded to the degree of Doctor of Medicine at Liverpool University; H. H. Carleton has taken the same degree at Oxford; F. Bracecamp has graduated at Cam- bridge; W. Maginn secured the seventh place in the Ckvil Service examination wider the junior appointments scheme, obtaining over 75 per cent, marks in Latin, French, and Greek. This was an excellent performance. J. A. G. Sterling has passed the final examination en- titling him to practice as a barrister at Lin- coln's Inn; C. Hayes has taken the first part of the examination of the same body in con- stitutional law and legal history; T. C. Locker has been successful in the associateship exam- ination of the Chartered Institute of Secre- taries; A. Lee has obtained honours in the in- termediate examination of the Society of In- corporated Accountants; C. H. Unsworth has passed the intermediate examination of the Law Society; G. Gladding has been elected to a scholarship of ,t. 50 for three years at Dalton Hall, Manchester University; and G. Wood- cock has obtained a fiCst clasp in the mathe- matical special, Part 1., at Cambridge. The school would most sincerely-congratulate these Old Boys on the success which has attended their several efforts.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0269.txt | included=true | included =====
The School Games.

There is no necessity for me to speak at length on the part played by school games; no innovations have been introduced. The camp of last summer holidays, held in Derby- shire, under the superintendence of Mr. Pear- son, was pronounced a great success. So de- lighted were the lrys with, their experience that the wish was expressed early in rthe present term that a similar camp should be held this year. This has been arranged, and the boys, under Mr. Pearson's charge, set out on Friday. We hope they Will have a very enjoyable and profitable time. The staff, in which you, and I believe parents also, place implicit trust, has again worked most strenuously and unselfishly. I take this opportunity of publicly expressing my indebted- ness to them for their most hearty co-operation and support, as upon such help to myself the SUGAR Is DEARER.—Advt.

===== 0002601_19140801_art0270.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTATION OF PRIZES.

Sir Bowen Bowen-Jones, Chairman of the Shropshire County Council, said he was pleased to be present at such a function, more particu- larly us his old friend, Sir Gilbert Greenall, had asked him to attend.

Their school was doing a great educational work, and he was pleased to hear the record it held. (Applause.) Secondary education was very essential to the welfare of the country, and he was pleased to learn about what War- rington was doing in the matter: Endowed schools like the Warrington Gram- mar School had been the great pioneers of education, and on that ground alone they ought to receive the hearty support of the local educa- tion authority. He considerea that the object of educationists should be as far as possible to connect the old and valuable traditions of a school with the present requirements of the educational needs of the district. He was of the opinion that all endowed schools which had come under the Board of Education should re- ceive substantial support from the local authori- ties with regard to the staffing of the schools and the equipment. The training of the intellect was the least part of education. The formation of character was the most important factor educationists should hare in view.

Umbricius tells Davus to admit no one except Cordus, a poet. Davits is deaf and tells the doorkeeper to admit none but poets. He then goes to -fetch a workman to fix a lid to a chest. Several poets, who have been admitted, attempt to recite to Umbricins. During an interval he conceals himself in the chest. Davits returns with the workman and they begin to nail on the lid. Umbricius calls out, the workman hears, but Davus does not, and so insists on the work being continued. "The Taming of the Shrew."—Baptista, C. W. Healey; Petruchio, E. jeffery; Grentio, S. TYPEWRITING DEMONSTRATION. Remarkable Speeds by Female Operators.

All who attended the high-speed demonstra- tion at Cairo-street last Friday evening will agree that it was a remarkable event in the com- mercial history of this district. It is entirely due to the opening of Wood's Business College in Bold-street that Warrington was able to stage a demonstration upon such a large scale, in which, with one exception, all the competitors were local students of the College. Mr. J. R. Widdup, of Liverpool, officiated as Chairman, and in the course of his remarks urged students, in addition to acquiring mech- anical skill on the writing machine, to cultivate their general education, their natural apti- tudes, and to study the special features of the "business concern" that employed them. Miss ‘fillicent Woodward, champion typist of the United Kingdom, and first lady operator in the world's championship contest, gave a mar- vellous exhibition of high-speed touch typing. Copying from a "Liverpool Echo" handed to her by a member of the audience, she wrote accurately at a speed of 120 words per minute, and from dictation while blindfold, attained a still higher rate. The speed at which the Underwood Typewriter iS capable of working was proved when Miss Woodward wrote mem- orised matter at 197 words per minute. It should be noted that Miss Woodward oper- ated an ordinary Underivood machine, used by other students of the College. Miss Dorothy Swinton, the clever young War- rington operator, also gave a remarkable demon- stration. From unseen matter she typed 84 and 91 words per minute; memorised matter 180; and from dictation 95 words. Miss Swinton also operated an ordinary Underwood machine. It is a very decided benefit to the young people of Warrington to have such an educational es- tablishment available in their midst. It con- tinues to a much* higher and more practical stage the teaching of the ordinary day schools. and puts into the hands of a large majority of students the training, the encouragement, and the opportunity for earning a higher scale of remuneration than they could otherwise obtain. Prizes to the successful students were presented by Mrs. F. Wilson Wood as folloNa: Team cOntest AL Pilkington, El. Hough, E. Broadhurst, J. Motyneux, I. Cunnington, D. Howard, M. Ellis—box of chocolates. Shorthand prizes for best work displayed. Judge, J. I?. Widdup. 1, Miss Mabel Pilkington ; 2, Miss May Ellis; 3, Miss Annie Haslcm; 4, Miss Josephine Kilduff, Misses Woodward . and Swinton were each presented with a choice box of chocolates. BACON IS DEAHER.—Advt.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 1, 1914.

Everyone was of the opinion that the picnic had been one of the most enjoyable ever held. CROSFIELD•AND THE WORK TO BE DONE. The Memorial Ilall. Mrs. CROSFIELD was the first to address the large assembly after tea, and as she rose she was loudly cheered. She said she was astonished to learn that Blackpool, which had treated them so liberally in the matter of weather and glorious sunshine, was repretented by a Conaervative. It should

each one would do what she could for the Asso- ciation, it would not only be the most important Association in Lancashire and Cheshire—as it ' was—but the most important in Great Britain. (Applause.) She was pleased that many of them had been sporting the red feather; it was very splendid. (Laughter.) She hoped they and many more would always sport that feather, and at no time sport the blue or white feather. Then at the next election they would achieve the fine result at which they were aiming. MISS GREENALL'S WEDDING. Pretty Spectacle at Stockton Heath. CROWDS OF SPECTATORS. Stockton Heath was en fete on Tuesday. The occasion was the wedding of Miss Dorothy Helen Greenall, elder daughter of Mr. Henry Greenall, and the villagers entered into the matter with great zest. Flags were flown from many build- ings and the village practically held a holiday to mark the event. The ceremony took place in St. Thomas' Church, and there was a large congregation present, while outside a large crowd assembled. The entrance to the church was lined by a guard of honour from the Duke of Lancaster's Imperial Yeomanry, of which the bridegroom is an officer. The interior of the church was handsomely decorated, and much floral decora- tion was to be seen at the home of the bride. In a word the wedding was one of the prettiest seen in Stockton Heath. The bridegroom was Mr. William Piers Jones,

A GALLANT WARRINGTONIAN. A Plucky Feat is Recognised. Recognition by the Royal Humane Society of a very plucky feat in the River Mersey has been made when that Society presented its certificate for bravery to James Ball, a young man residing at 44, Cockhedge-lane, Warrington. It will be recalled that Mr. Ball on the 30th May plunged into the river and, at great peril, rescued a young woman who had thrown herself into the water. The Mayor (Councillor Peter Peacock) made the presentation of the certificate at the Police Court on Monday, and heartily congratulated Mr. Ball upon his brave act. He said that the

AN EARLESTOWN TRAGEDY. • Coroner and a Mother. Coroner Brighouse had something to say to a mother concerned in an inquest he held on Mon- day afternoon. The inquiry was on an eight months old child named Gladys Greeves. who was found dead in bed through strangulation caused by a cord from a "dummy." The mother, Emily Greeves, aged 21 years, who lives in London-row, Vulcan, said after she had put the child and another named Frank to bed, she locked the door and went out shopping. The Coroner (Mr. Brighouse): We•have had nine children at our house, and I am a grand- father to ten, so I know what I am talking about and how children ought to be looked after. Had the children comforters in their mouths? Witness: Yes, sir. The Coroner: If I had my way I would shoot the man who invented these things. I fear I am a very old-fashioned father, and perhaps my wife is an old-fashioned mother. The ways of the present generation are not like the genera- tion when we brought up our children. My wife would no more have dreamt of leaving her children in the house than the man in the moon. I simply hate and, abominate these com- forters. They do a lot of harm, and the Legisla- ture ought to step in and say there ought to be no comforters. The jury, after consultation in private, re- turned a verdict of "Accidental death, due to strangulation." The Coroner (to the mother): I have informa- tion in my possession that entitles me to say that you have been asking for this. I know for a fact that you have left these children locked up in the house by themselves while you went to Warrington for the evening. The Mother: Never, sir. The Coroner: We will have it thrashed Out. The Coroner then called P.C. Brothwell. The Coroner: Is it true that you have hjtcl complaints from peOple that this mother 7 has left the children in the house and gone to War- rington? The Constable: Yes, sir. I saw her going by herself last night.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. The open meetinr, of the Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday was presided over by Mr. B. Farrar, who gave a short report upon the work of the Larcashire Federation Conference recently held at Hyde. ' The Rev. T. T. James, in giving his monthly ad'hess, spoke upon "A Certain Ministry." We often thought ourselves useless, he said, because we could do no special work. This was not so, for we could all do something in our own way. We could all speak a word of encourage- ment, gladdening hearts that were heavy and sad, instead of discouraging others after an effort of service. "Let the Words of my mouth," said Harriet Beecher Stowe upon one occasion, "and the meditation of my heart be acceptable to thee, 0 God." She was a woman who never failed to ask God's guidance in her speech or silence; daily she prayed that she might never find fault except for some good purpose, and though she wished to be slow in perceiving the faults of others, their goodness she was anxious not to fail to praise. When Christ bade Zacchfens to "come down," He was working upon the good in the man, and not troubling about his failings. This He (lid with every man, and so every man should seek out the good in those around him. The older man should not soil the hearts of the boys in the workshop, which it was very easy to do. He should always be ready both with the word CLARKE'S 841 PILLS can be re:. ti and effort of reconciliation, speaking at all charges in eitherfrom iga uFlaaCr(lllgedano r cer:t.vieti times that which Christ desired him to speak. in the back. WORKS. BOWLERS. ,Success of the Wire Ropery Team. MAYORESS PRESENTS PRIZES. It was a great final in the Works Association Competition on Saturday, in spite of the fre- quent showers which rendered the green very heavy. A pleasing and fitting termination to a gigantic and successful competition was that the Mayoress should present the prizes at the close, and that speeches of great interest were made by the Mayor, Mr. A. THE SPOONER CUP. Rylands' Win the Third Round. Rylands continue to make a splendid fight in the Spooner Cup Competition, and on Satur- day, although they did not bat as well as usual, they defeated Tranmere Victoria by 19 runs. Their top scorer was J. Waywell, who knocked up a nice 23 before he fell to a tricky ball bowled by Phillips, S. Carter scored 16 by good play, and F. Horrocks came next with 10, and he was also a victim of Phillips. Rylands were out for 69, and they got their rivals all out for 50. Phillips was the mainstay of the Tranmere team and took six wickets for 21 runs, while he batted carefully. McGowan took four for 15, and Carter three for 14 for the winners. FRIENDLY MATCHES.
